• " C A C rrA L" AT.., -- There is a struggle of gigantic dinu-nsions ahead of the labour world. Inspired in a gi'eat measure by the success of the employers in the engineering strike, the employers in various departments of industry are being organised. If the organisation can take the shape of a national federation of large employers, this will be the form given to it. If it cannot be made national, then the movement will assume the form of groups or combinations of cor- related and mutually-dependent industries, the ob- ject of which will be to break down the men's unions one by one. It is believed in labouring circles that the struggle is not very far distant. Tne cfmbination of employers has made considerable progress, and there is little doubt that if it is not a national combination at first it will assume the form of a national federation of groups of em- ployers before long. At any rate, all the combina- tions of employers in mutually-connected trades will be in close sympathy and touch with the other combinations,and working men or their leaders can- not too soon begin to consider how they are to meet this dan.er. The account given by the witl,Av of the Claimant of the manner in which the so-called confession was obtained from him opens a new sensation in the case. Under a promise made to him at a moment when he was starving and in ill-health, to pay him £3OOO it he would sign the name "Arthur Orton" to a story of his life written by someone else,in which he was made to confess that he was not fichborne, he yielded to the temptation, and, according to his widow's narration,showed an extraordinary amount: of simplicity in the business part oh the transaction, for he believed that the person who negotiated with him did so under a false name, and he part ed with the document without getting the £3OOO in ex- change for it. All he received was less than £4OO, which is said to have come from the weekly paper which published it, and this journal ought now to have some light to throw on the transaction. What the individual who negotiated with the deceased obtained is unknown to the widow. The confession, it would taus seeni,was not the result of contrition an the Claimant's part,but another great hoax upon the public for a sordid end.

There is some commotion in the theatrical warld of London at the present moment at the prospect of the creation of a great theatrical trust, wit h enorm- ous capital, which is to do for the dramtvic amuse- ment of the public what some gigantic hotel com- panies have done for its entertainment in other directions. I rather think the company is already formed and at work, and a new opera, which has just been produced in Dublin, is generally said to have been brought out under its auspices. The fact that it has broken ground in Dublin shows that it is not a mere London speculation, and I understand one of its objects is to buy or bvild theatres in pro- vincial towns, and to produce plays, especially new plays, in the houses owned by the syndicate. This trust is said to have a capital of half a million at its back, but I fear it will not see much return for its money. Most towns in the provinces of any pre- tensions are probably already supplied with as many theatres as the town can maintain, and in London there is a bran new and elegant and well- conducted theatre in every suburb.

An interesting but little-known scheme for allot- ment purchase,' learn, has been in succe-sful opera- tion on Tyneside for about seven years. The scheine is, in effect,an application of the co-operative prin- ciple in acquisition and distribution of land. Estates are partitioned into small areas and retailed at wholesale prices. In this way seven more or less important estates—representing 800 acres—have been distributed among 249 proprietors; 116 new dwelling-houses have been erected or are in course of erection;and the increase in the population'of the localities in which the holdings are situated is said to be 600. That all classes are represented is shown from tne feet that the houses vary in value from £l5O to £3,000. These results are a sufficient indi- cation of success;and while the enterprise is carried' out on a sound commercial basis, it is obviously none the less effective as an a,,ency of social re- form. The membership of this allotment society —the iiability of individuals being limited to a small subscription —numbers 715. with the addition uf two co-operative societies. It has not hitherto taken advantage of the Allotment Acts, nor shown any desire to enlist the support of the social philan- thropist. "It expresses neither approval nor dis- approval of the existing tenure of land, but, with- , out prejudice to the opinions of its members there- ' on, seeks to make the best of things as they are, leaving ulterior issues to politicians."

The Kitchen Committee are treating the action of Sir Wilfrid Lawson in raising the question of the sale of intoxicating liquor in the precincts of the Hoarse of Commons by an action in the courts of law rather indifferently. They consider the matter in the light of a good joke, and, in spite of the re- cant admissions of the Attorney General, some of the Committee appear inclined to take the view that no cow t would attempt to exercise jurisdiction within the Palace of Westminster. It seems pro- bable that the threatened proceedings,if carried out will be directed in the first instance against the sale of drink not to members but to "strangers." who now obtain refreshments at the bar in the Central Hall, Sir Richard Webster has the matter under his consideration at the present tinao,an.d it is of course by his advice that the Committee will eventually be guided.

-- - Spurgeon's Tabernacle, in Newington Causeway, London, which was ilestreyed by fire on Wednes- dy, was in itself a tribute to the power and vigorous personality of the great Nonconformist preacher for whom it was built, and whose name naturally usurped its more formal title of the " Metropolitan " Tabernacle. When Spurgeon became pastor of the New Pirk-street Chapel, Southwark, out of which the Tabernacle grew, he was under twenty years of age. But in a few mo.litha the chapel, which had been nearly empty hefore, could not contain the congregations that flocked to hear him A lerger chapel was built only to be found quite inadequate, and in 1856 when Spurgeon was only twenty-two. it was decided to build a great Tabernacle to seat six thousand persons. It was while the Tabernacle was being built and Spurgeon was preaching in the Surrey Gardens Music Hall—s place that held ten thousand people—that someone maliciously raised a cry of fire and caused a panic which resulted in the death of seven persons and the injury of many others. The Tabernacle was opened in 1861, and it va- enough to make the building famous that Sunday after Sunday for more than thirty years Spurgeon filled it at every service. Few Noncon- formists or Americans viLiLed London for the first time without going as a matter of course to hear Fpurgeon at the ft,hernacle. The news of the des- truction of the building will be received with regret In many parts of the English-speaking world.

Mr. Davitt is contributing a series of articles in one of the London morning papers, describing his observations on the management of the convict Prisons during a round of vieits he has just made to them. Partly as a result of this, and partly from his extensive knowledge and study of the subject, has begun to put down a number of amend- ments to the Prisons Bill, all in the direction of the more humane treatment of prisoners. These will be followed by others on the same lines, and as the Home Secretary, throughout the discussion on the secord reading of the Bill, manifested a great amount of open•mindedness and a strong disposition towards at least a more reasonable treatment of prisoners, it may be taken for granted that not few of Mr. Davitt's amendments will be embodied CM the new Legislative Prieon Code.

The curl ;us part of the matter is tha;:. large number—the great majority in fact— werk with Sitting Bull at the massacre of General Custer.

The shadow of mundane war does not seem to slacken the energy of the Salvationists in their spiritual cam- paign in America. General Booth began on Monday a week's visit to New York. To this end preparations bad been made for some time,and the opening ceremony wa, in Carnegie Hall,although most of the other meetings of the week took place in the Balvation Army buildiv..o Wftt nurteenth-street. Oa Saturday afternoon there to i:e an open-air rally in Union-square, and on Sunday, General Booth will address two meetings in the Acaderc* of Music, one in •he afternoon and one in the evenini, while on the 26:h, the day of the closing of the week's exercises,there will be a great meeting in the Metropoli- tan Opera House, at which General 13enjamin F. Trac:. his promised to preside.

The three Cycling Cornmissiorers of "Travel." Messrs. Fraser, Lunn, and Lowe, who are now crossing thp Rockies and Western America riding full speed for horn' have had a most interesting interview with the Marqui,, Ito during their stay in Japan. The Prime Minister invite,' them t* dinner at his official residence at Tokio, to meet the other Japanese Ministers of State, Mr. Fraser, who records the interview was much impressed with the quiet optimism of the Japanese Premier. He regp rds the Matquis as a man who, whilst a leader in reform, is really also a retraining force. The cyclists have now covered 15,000 miles in their ride from England to Yokohama, and have still three thousand miles to traverse across the American Continent. They expect to reach London towards the end of July.

The barbers of New York have noticed a decide change in the matter of moustaches since war has be,n in the air. Hitherto the fashion has been for small, well- trimmed moustaches, but now, it is stated, every man Who can grow a heavy, ferocious, military-lookii-, moustache is doing so, and all sorts of devices are bdog resorted to give as much as possible the appearance of what Colman in a particular case once described as a " shoe-brush stuck beneath the nose." A similar effect was noticed in Germany just before and during the Franco-German war, and evidences of the same feeling have been noticed in other countries, and even in some of our own regiments.

A statistic.an, basing his conclusions upon the average increase of insanity during the past forty years, has reached the conclosion that by the year 2301 there will be no further use for insane asylums, as there will not be sane persons enough to lock up the crazy ones. The old Quaker was still more of a pessimist when in a con- fidential mood he said to his wife : " Bettie, the whole world is off its balance, but thee and me ; and now I look a little more closely, thee seems a little peculiar tie." It was a gratification to the old man that there was at least one sane man in the world.

President McKinley is thus described by a correspon- dent who knows him well :—" He was born in Ohio in 1844, and is !hsrefore still in the prime of life. He is very tall and stoutly built. His features are regular and his eyes exceedingly flue, and although he is a very big man, he has a singular resemblance to Napoleon 1., especially when he is seated in is chair, and deep in thought. He is by no means a wealthy MAO, having lost much of his money ten years ago in a speculation which turned out the reverse of succes,ful. No hardet-work- ing man exists than the President, who, like the Pope, rise at six every morning. He does not care for play, and his greatest relaxation is the reading of novels, a pastime in which he often indulges after dinner. He married, thirty years ago, MiAs Ida Saxon, of Canton, who is a very accomplished and agreeable woman."

The Leicestershire co'liery disaster is in many ways the most painful that mai happened in England for a very long time. Of an explosion er a falling-in of work- ings or a sudden outbre.k of deadly gases one an usually say at least that it brought death quickly. But toe thirty-five men who were killed on Tuesday were entrapped by a " gob fire " that broke out a mile and a half away from them, and had to wait hopelessly for the end until the flames had exhausted the air and the dense smoke had spread tar en mgh to complete the slow work of suffocation. Only lie, out of forty men managed to break through the deadly barrier and escape, and one of these at least must have felt more grief than joy in his deliverance. The story of the father and eon who ran hand-in-hand for their lives until the one dropped exhausted or was pulled down in the panic and the other had no strength to help him on, and the fate of the two brave lads who ran trick to warn their comrades of the danger and so lost their own chance of safety—these are but two examples of the many tragic incidents and her.ic actions which always go to make up what we call a great colliery accident.

The newly-elected president of the Society of En- gineera (Mr. W. B. Beaumont), has raised a plea for the improvement of our road surfaces, particularly in the country. At present, according to the report by the Rev. Or. Gritton in the " British Architect," there are about 1,400,000 draught horses at work in the United Kingdom. Of these at least one-tenth could be easily dispensed with by small improvements upon the present methods of constructing and maintaining our roads. To save onlv onP-tonth of 500,000 horses would mean a sav- ing of £1,500,000 per ye4r, and the remaining 450,000 would be ahle to earn frcm £5 to £lO per year more than at present. At £5 a year the saving en this head alone would be £2,2so,ooo,making a total of 0.750.000. This sum capitalised at 3 per cent., represents £125,000,000. It is obvious, therefore, at least £100,000.000 sterling of national money might be pro- fitably Invested in the improvements of highways. INHUMAN W.RAPONS OF WAR.

FEARFUL RESULTS OF BRITISH BULLETS. At the German Surgical Conferesei in Wiesbaden the well-known professor of surgery, Doctor von Bruns, of Tiitingen, road a paper on "Inhuman Weapons of vvar,". which has had much attention drawn to it throughout Germany. Doctor von Bruns has made a number of experiments on dead bodies or portions of them with the bullets supplied to the British troops dur- ini recent campaign in the North-Wert of India. He says that the result,' of the contact of these bullets with the human body are fearful, and cannot be exaggerated. Not only are the bones, but the flesh as well, torn and splintered in all directious. Eyed the skin at the point of contact shows long seams torn in all directions. Doctor von Bruns expressed the hope that the German Government would take steps to bring about an agreement among the European Powers for the exclusive Wl^. of small calibre weapons supplied with bullets which would be either entirely Covered with lead or have at least the points protected with this metal. The bullet should only render the ha t interest. With feelings of sup:rife she had noted Dennis Vane's absence from court. At the hearing before, the magistrates he had figured conspicuously, always in company with Eric Hume. If Dennis Vane, the man in whose goodness and judgment she had unbounded confidence, believed her guilty of the crime of which she stood accused, was it possible that the jury, who judged her alone by the evidence placed before them, would acquit her? But one answer could arise in response. He was a tall, thin man, with a quick,- observant' manner. He had occupied his present post for fifteen years, and was trusted implicitly by his employers. The art of never uttering a thoughtless or unnecessary word, which he had cultivated in his youth, had materially assisted him in gaining his present position, and bore him in good stead at this juncture. CIIAPTER XXXII.

It was the first day of Gvvendoline Treherne's trial. The Central Criminal Court was crowded with elegantly-attired wonen. All knew that it would be a fashionable trial, one which would monopolise the attention of society and provide discussable matter for dinner-parties. In fact, so keen was the interest displayed therein that the Court presented quite a pleasing study in pretty summer costumes, fans and scent-bottles figuring as conspicuously as at a fashionable church. "I told you, Violet, how crowded the court would be," observed old Colonel Frossart, turning to his daughter. "We ought not to have come. Ido not approve of this court being turned into a fashionable rendezvous, when a trial which reflects so much discredit in certain quarters, is to be heard."

"But, father, I wanted so much to see Miss Treherne in the dock. I promised to tell the Lathorns how she looked and behaved. The last time I saw her was at the Royal Academy private view, when everyone was making such a ridiculous fuss over her." "Though I have little or no pity for the girl, your curiosity to see her in her present position appears to me positively heartless. Don't you comprehend how painful it must be for her to know she is the centre of attraction, in such a place as this? With her artistic, sensitive temperament it must be nothing short of torture. Honestly speaking, I do not like it being known that we are here. It's setting a bad example to others." "But Lady Dyke-Thomas and the De Vera- Granges and the Stiltons are all here." "That doesn't make our conduct less contemp- tible. I wish I had vetoed your coming. Ala! here she comes—poor girl!" The ravages which protracted anxiety had made in Gwendoline Treherne's clever, beautiful face could not fail to elicit pity even in the most callous-hearted. As she took her place in the dock a general feeling of commiseration was dis- cernable among the public. She glanced round nervously, intuitively realising that the crowded condition of the court, and the presence of many fashionable people, was directly due to the fact that her trial was about to take place. "They have all come to look at me! " she mentally exclaimed with a shudder. Meanwhile the Judge had taken his place on the Bench and the indictment was being read out by the clerk. Counsel on both sides were still poring over documents and holding hurried com- munications with their solicitors. Gwendoline tried to concentrate her whole attention on the indictment; but with curious irrelevance she found her eyes taking note of matters in no way connected with her trial. One moment an old lady, levelling a pair of opera-glasses at her, distracted her attention ; the next she discovered herself adversely criticising the mixture of gaudy colours in a hat near her. The movement of the many fans, the heated atmosphere of the court, and the know- ledge that she was regarded as a public spectacle, jarred upon her overstrung nerves, and accentuated the difficulty of keeping her thoughts in the groove she desired.

At last the introductory technicalities were over, and the first witness, Richard Musgrove, took his place in the witness-box. Again it flashed across Gwendoline's mind that his appearance was familiar to her. She proceeded to mentally criticise his features, manners, &c., wondering where and when she had seen him. When under examination his demeanour was even more convincing than it had been at the hearing before the magistrates. •He emphatically corroborated all that he had then stated, and added weight thereto by giving additional details. "Were you surprised at the accused asking a total stranger like yourself to cash a cheque ? " said Sir Edward Masters in his cross-examination. "Yes ; I was." "Why did you not decline to cash it ? " "I did, at first." "What induced you to it ultimately ? " "Because accused stated that her uncle, Sir Stewart Bathurst, had taken a house in the locality, and that he might become a customer of mine., " Gwendoline started and ilushed to the temples at the mention of her uncle's name; and Sir Edward Masters turned to Eric Hume and asked a ques- tion, evidently with reference to the witness's last statement. The answer corroborated the fact that Sir Stewart Bathurst had taken a house as described. Counsel therefore at once dismissed the subject. "Describe the dress worn by accused on this occasion," he asked. "It was a blue dress." "What shade of blue ? "

"I have heard it de-scribed as—" "I don't want to hear how you have heard it described by others. Give me your own descrip- tion of it."

Musgrove thought for a moment, then answered: "It was the kind of blue you see on plums." "I never saw a blue plum," interrupted the Judge. "It was a plum-blue, my lord," added the witness good-humouredly. "Was the dress trimmed ? " asked Sir Edward. "Yes, with curious-looking gold braid, very narrow, and laid on in a strange, twisting kind of pattern." "How long did accused remain in the shop ? " "About ten minutes."

"At what hour of the day did she call?" "Thu clock was striking three as she left." Here counsel took up the small pocket-book in. which various entries occurred in reference to the cashing of cheques. "I see you have mentioned Wednesday as the day of the week on which you cashed the cheque. Are you quite certain it was on a Wednesday? " "Quite," replied witness, with emphasis. "What was the day of the month? "The 15th of July." "Would you be surprised to hear that the 15th of July fell on a Tuesday last year?" "I should," replied witness, colouring. "It did, nevertheless." "I suppose I must have been mistaken witness proceeded to explain. "No explanation, sir!" thundered counsel. "Everyone is liable to make mistakes!" hotly retorted Musgrove. "Do not presume to argue with counsel! " observed the Judge severely. "You must restrict yourself to answering questions." Musgrove looked from the Judge to Sir Edward with considerable trepidation, and moved his hands nervously. Sir Edvrard eyed him with an expression that revealed he was conscious of having "scored one " for his side; then he took up the pocket-book and carefully examined it. "How long have you had this note-book in your possession?" he asked. _

"About two yea;;,-; far as I can remember." "The first entry is; ' Paid Tollington seven shillings and threepence. Half-share.' What does this refer to? " "Tollington is a friend of mine. We had been no the river together. The expenses came to seven shillings and threepence each." "When did this excursion take place?" "The summer before last." "When you bought this pocket-book, had you the intention of entering particulars in it of the cheques that you cashed ? " "No. But a friend of mine, who got into trouble by cashing a forged cheque, advised my. noting down particulars of cheques that I suspected might not be honoured, or that I cashed for strangers." "Is it your practice to cash cheques which yot think may not be honoured ? " 'No." "No! You said jut now that you noted down particulars of cheques which you suspected might not be honoured. Explain this statement." Witness shuffled about nervously for a few moments. Then he observed lamely: "I sometimes cashed cheques that I did not feel over-certain about." "But you cashed them all the same?" "Yes; if I thought it would lead to business." "Do you frequently risk sumtiL,, for•instance,. forty pounds in the , hopes ,nluigini your con-._ nection."

"No." "Why did you feel justified in doing so in thii instance? "

"She said No,' as the family was just leaving England for a few weeks." A hurried colloquy ensued. Hanks and Co., bankers, was of a technical nature. He spoke as to the time when the book containing the cheque was supplied to Mr. Vane, and with regard to other matters that concerned the bank. This closed the evidence for the prosecution, and the defence having no witnesses to call, counsel for the prosecution began his summing up. He commenced by stating that his clients had been aware for several months who had uttered the forged cheque, but that they had felt the strongest disinclination to prosecute the accused, believing that she had uttered it not knowing that it was forged. Her excellent position, both in the artistic and social world, seemed to preclude the possi- bility of her having committed the crime of which she now stood accused. He need scarcely say that in bringing this trial his clients had acted entirely from a sense of duty, especially painful under the circumstances, and it would have been a source of satisfaction had the accused been able to bring forward refuting evidence. However, what was law for the poor was law for the rich; and the fact that accused occupied a position in which temptation to dishonesty could not exist, was no reason that, in face of strong evidence to the contrary, she should be regarded as beyond the possibility of erring. He did not wish to say one word that might cause her, or her relatives, additional pain. The position in which she was 'Sow placed must be so acutely humiliating, to a .rcon of her position, that he would avoid as ....101l as possible adding thereto. It was a mystery-enveloped case. flow ver, the jury must bear in mind that they we not called upon to deal with ,the unravelling of mysteries, but with the plain facts as placed before ttera. It had been a hard matter for Gwendoline to listen in silence to the evidence of the five wit- nesses, hut it was many degrees haidik to remain silent, When counsel eloquently explained his deduc- tions therefrom. Carefully did he hread his way through the 'facts of the ease. VimitlY he enlarged upon the evidence of the witness, .4ichard Moe- grove. It was impossible, he said,- 'Co 'doubt that: the accused waif in possessiore of information regarding this witness's position. It must be remembered that' previous to asking him to cash the cheque she had applied ,uns,necesgfillly,, in two quarters. It Could therefore lie'safely presUmed that she,xlecided, or 'Was 'urgeci, ‘l4y a third person— fort to bleer lled no con- federates—to take a higher hand in the matter,, and to represent that the cashing of this cheque would indirectly benefit the person who did so. The witness, Richard Musgrove, fell an easy prey to n inducement, which promised to improve his

faiuon bile was evideiY ,Li assure, trom sum wines: the. lacked customers; and that a promise to mention his name to her uncle, Sir Stewart Bathurst, would prove especially alluring. la short, ,taking the witne.ss's cliarucLer into amounts, the very elements were present that would render him easily imposed upon by a clever and discerning women: The witness had given his evidence in a straightforward manner, which carried with it weight and conviction. In spite of not being what is generally termed a good business man, he was capable of exercising a considerable amount of judgment; and this had led him to accept a friend's advice and write down the numbers and ' Particulars of those cheques which haf Mishedifor people; of whom he had only a limited knowlage. It might naturally be said that only in imprudent man would cash cheques under such circumstances; but circumstances sometimes pointed to the wisdom of 'running commercial,risks. His learned brother had attempted to.threw doubt on the value Of this part of the evidence, and had called attention to the fact that there were entries rtagarding a cheque drawn by one of her Majesty's Ministers and cashed by the witness. However, the witness had fully explained the: matter by stating that his doubts, whether the cheque .would be honoured, were grounded on a rumour that the right honour- able gentleman had 'met with a fatal accident:. - Next, counsel referred to the weight of evidede that must naturally attach to the description of the dress which accused had, worn on this 15th of July. The jury had beard the colour of the fabrics fully described by the various witnesses. Tt was a 'remarkable fact, that there was no discrepancy in their descriptions. The dress Was neither striking= ' leoking nor . gaudy, but one which would' attract notice for its tasteful harmony- of colouring..; The trimming, however, was its most • remarkable feature. This was acknowledged to• be ''tiiriqbe, having been made expressly. . for accused bentder of a relative, during his •temporary sojnarn, in Algeria, and was introduced after a design Of—Ur own. Unfortunately, the dress itself could not be put in as evidence, as it no longer exisied, in its original form. Had it not been for the 'Striking features in Connection with it, the accused might never have been brought to justice. It was impoiet sible to believe, for a moment, that another dress existed exactly similar to the one in pieetios Thomas Brown's evidence on the subject was especially important, as he was able to describe the trimming minutely by reason of his having resided in Algeria, and being also conversant with colours and artistic designs. Then counsel dwelt upon the - importance' of accused's statement to Richard Musgrove, with reference to Sir Stewart Bathurst having taken a furnished house in the locality. This, he con- tended, pointed incontestibly to accused's identity. The 'witness knew nothing of her family, and no other, means existed, by which be could have Nina that Sir Stewart Bathurst had taken this house, especially as it had not been inhabited by him, owing to illness. The knowledge, therefore; that he had ever intended living there must necessarilf have been limited to a few. To another" witness, Henry Trent, accused had also made a remark 'which pointed to her identity. This 'remark had reference to her approaching visit to Switzerland. After drawing" the attention of the Jury to , further facts, to be' gather,.(l from the evidence, Mr. Vernon, Q. C., ceased abruptly to speak. Then in a low voice, full of emotion, he added: "Heaven -forbid•that I should say one unnecessary word to influence you against the accused. I have -tried,- so far •as lies in my power, to place the evidence for the prosecution explicitly and fell* before you. My duty has been a painful one, and it has been with sincere regret that I have been compelled to deduce facts that point to the guilt of one so highly gifted as the accused; who has not only gained the love and respect of those with whom she has been most intimately connected, but stands high in the estimation of her fellow. countrymen and women." Throughout Mr. Vernon's able speech Gwendo• line had not once lifted her eyes from the ground. The deadly pallor of her face reflected her mental suffering, and as counsel uttered these last remarks her lips quivered, and she retained her self command with difficulty. (To be continued.)

LADIES' LETTER

MY DEAR NELLIE, Hats for spring wear are decidedly smaller than they were last spring, and the circular shape with dented front is exceedingly fashionable. Complete Wreaths of flowers and foliage will be worn, with b3wB and fans of lace, chiffon, or kilted glace silk, and many of the fronts have a very wide, volumin- -008 bow of glace silk, with flowers heaped up at the back, and spreading over the crown. Toques are still popular, and these and straw hats in vivid Shades are veiled with spotted or sequined tulle.

Panama sailor hats will be much worn, with folds of rose-pink silk and filmy yellowish 'see, draped round the crown, and caught down here and there with pearl pins, while at the left side there 58a bow of black velvet and some black wigs all a- glitter with jet paillette.s. Nothing could be pret- tier, and any colour can of course be introduced in ' the silk—turquoise-blue, pea-green, orange, white, atd so on. Many of the new hats are turned up excessively at the back, and the flowers held up with empire cot.ribs, principally of jet. These fasten into the Coil of hair, and the front simply requires one hat. Pin to keep it firm. The high comb forms the extreme back of the hat, and has the effect of being a part of the coiffure.

Tucks figure largely on silk shirts, and run their course from neck to waist, while there are little double frills of the mauve over white passing down the front, and finishing the cuffs, and a band of White silk at the neck with a smart cravat bow of kilted silk. A black silk dress, trimmed with tiny ruchinge of mixed black and ecru Valenciennes lace, about one inch in depth, is absolutely original. The two faces are simply gathered together as fall as pos- sible, sewn on the dress, and pulled in and out to give a regular effect. On the skirt these niches ran round at intervals of three inches, as high as the knee, and she skirt being cut with a good deal of " spring " at the edge, gives the effect of a eircular flounce, covered with narrow ruchings.

„Tailor-made cloth costumes are more elaborate season than they have ever been before. here is a good deal of beautiful workmanship now Pat into stylish walking costumes. The coat fits in clo•ely to the figure at the aides, and the whole costume is lined througb With cream silk. "WOMEN'S CHAT."

Princess Charles ol ammark, who is now with her mother, the Princess of Wales, and her favouiN, sister, Princess Victoria. at Sandringham, is one of the few members of the Royal family who are quite familiar with Russian. She is devoted to reading, and is particularly loud of the works of the late Lord Lytton. But with all her love for intellectual purouite,Princess Charles pos- sesses a truly feminine lave for dress. She is very par- ticular about her clothes, and at any assembly is always one of the smartest turned out women to be seen. The West End of London is all bustle and preparation ler the season,now so close at hand. Shutters and blinds have disappeared,many of the balconies are one mass of sweet spring fi mere and painters are everywhere. One cannot but regret though, in the matter of outdoor dec- oration, that more uniformity in the stucco streets and passsages is not insisted upon. These as usual, are breaking out into a variegated mass of cnloring,as many hued as the most rainbow-like of Easter eggs. It ought not to be too much to expect that such colours as brilli- ant red and bright pink should not be placed side by side, or blue, and green, and yet these outrages may be seen each year, until the charitable smoke and dirt of winter merges them all with a neutral tint. Spring cleaning is the order of the day, but happily , the yearly "turn out" is no longer the formidable busi- ness it used to be in ancient times. The majority of women no longer consider it necessary to have every carpet up, every room papered, and whitewashed, and perchance painted at the same time which is indeed ate item to be thankful for. Despite our improved methods in this direetinn however, a few hints regarding details may not be superfluous. For instance,some of my readers may not be aware that nothing cleans silk so well as flannel. The careful housewife should always have silk covered furniture dusted with flannel, and all silk cur- tains, after being shaken, should be rubbel with flannel dusters. When walls are not cleaned down with bread or dough, they should be swept, and then rubbed with dusters, as the amount of dirt which escapes the broom Is consiberable. Paint should be washed from the floor upwards. If commenced from the top, some of the soapy water is sure to run down, and it is, frequently, almost impossible to remove these marks. There is an old fashioned idea, and I wish it were more generally remembered, " to make a house whole- some the attics should be kept " glean, and the cellars dry." In many houses which are otherwise well-regu- lated, and very tidy, lubbish of all sorts from old boots and shoes and bottles, to furniture, is stored in the attic in preparation for a turnine out day,and a bonfire, which alas ! all toe seldom comes off ! It is impossible to cal- culate how much dirt and unwholesomeness drifts down into the house from this neglected spot, and the conse- quence is that it is difficult to keep the rest of the house free from dust There is nothing more absurd really, than this hording of trash, and all that is needed to dis- perse it is a long mornings' work of sorting, and then the attic should be whitewashed and thoroughly cleansed I should remind my readers that a Single woollen scarf, or pair of stockings left lying about for a long while in an attic may alone bring enough moths to proves plague to the whole house, In all its bravery of colored tiles and marbles, shining brass, or bunt ironwork, the modern fireplace is truly a "thing of beauty " etc., and needs no decoration, Bet the modern fireplace does not fall to the lot of every pwoosmasnee'd of the L fa b: ogldu-estslli ion just now among those se oned specimens with their black surroundings, is, how beat to brighten or conceal arrangements. The latter mode of procedure women are taking away the tilise peusghlyeps the hest. Mu; fender or curb, putting the rug close up to the front and placing a small screen, painted or otherwise; in front of the actual grate. As a shade for the empty grate frames of Japanese parasols are again being utilised, covered with silk embroidery. I have seen several quite hand- some squares of work used in this way, and the owners thereof professed themselves as delighted to have found a use far a very useless article. Certainly gold and silk embroideries are difficult to arrange, not being, at present, fashionable as sofa cushions. Painted materiale, for evening wear, are being shown this season, but needlework is really far more satisfac- tory, especially the pretty ribbon embroidery which Marie Antoinette so greatly favoured. Bunches of flowers worked in these narrow ribbon, are united be true lovere' knots in gold thread, the pattern elaborated and cbarmingly jewelled with floral paillettee, sweet pees and guelder roses finding most favour. One of the very latest inventions is a veil fastener—a species of metal clasp, which is rather ornamental. Through this the ends of the net are drawn, the advan- tage being, a neat finish to the back of a hat, toque or bonnet. Few women are blessed with the knack of ar- ranging their veils artistically. Untidy individuals leave ends hanging out at the back of more or less damaged net; very neat women generally drag their veils too tightly across the forehead, thus allowing no graceful freedom to the hair, and destroying the look of eciftness which should distinguish it ; while the majority manage semehow to pinion their nose, the result being, Incess- ant fidgetting. • pretty gift that can be worked is one of the new veil cases,the satin embroidered cover bearing the words " Within this dainty case, Are filmy veils for thy sweet face." They are simply strips of cardboard long eneugh to take the veil, united by ribbons. Sachets for all purposes—gloves, handkerchiefs, and ties—mast all be scented, and are made twice as large as they used to be. Moreover, the more intricate the monogram, the better they NI in with the views of to-day. Before wo were permitted to despatch four ounces of letter for a penny,to decide upon the weight of an en- velope was a matter frequently involving the judgment of the whole houeehold. Mat is,of course,in the absence ef a letter balance, and it is perfectly marvellous bow few people there are who dream of possessing letter scales. One method of computing it was,to employ the kitchen scales, which unhappily are often superior to mere aecuracyor they decline to put in motion by any- thing less than half a pound. But as nothing ever does weigh more than four ounces, all anxiety is past and complexities of life in this direction almost over. Croquet promises to be very popular this rummer. • good deal was done last year,and a fair number of tour- naments held, but now the game seems really likely to enjoy a new lease of life. In some parts of the country , it is being played in most scientific style,on a small lawn It ot much bigger thee a billiard table.where no flippancy is allowed, and where the incompetent and the strong armed are at a dissount. Tennis, too, is not likely ti suffer much neglect. But girls should bear in mind the fact that jumpine at high ball., wide stretches, and violent runs are unsightly. A set of four, two men and two girls, is the ideal team. The girls are not then called upon to over-exert them- selves, and get dishevelled and over-heated, or perferm feats, which, if clever, are also ugly. To make old polished furniture look like new it must be trot clammed with vinegar, freely diluted with water. Then when all dirt has been removed apply the follow- ing polish with a rag and rub briskly. One gill et oil, one gill of vinegar, belt a gill of methylated spirit. This extremely simple operation performed once a week, will gradually produce a polish that is unrivalled. Boiling water may even be poured ever it w'th Impunity. It is not readily scratched and the wood having the pores filled with the application, becomes very bard, and is able to resist the attack of insects. Rhubarb Charlotte.—Butter a baking dish, and cover the bottom an inch deep with breaderumbs, then with a layer of rhubarb that has been cut Into thin small pieces Scatter this with sugar, and cover with a mond layer of crumbs. Arrange little knobs of butter over each layer of crumbs. Continue to fill the dish thus to the top, the top being crumbs. Bake in a slow even for an hour.

LOCAL DIVORCE CASES.

ELOPEMENT WITH A WIDNES SKIPPER.

In the Divorce Dividlon on Tuesday, before Mr. Justice Barnes, a case was heard in which Mr. Albert Edward Parry, an upholsterer, living in Chaster, sued for a divorce from his wife on the ground of her adul- tery with a co-respondent named Ge3rge Gandy, captain on a boat belonging to Widnes. There was no defence. Evidence was given to show that the parties were mar- ried on February 301. h, 1896, at Ethyl, the resnonden"s maiden name being Emma Evans. Petitioner's employ- ment being at Chester, he was absent from home from Monday till Saturday each week. Oa the 9th October, when he returned home, he found that his wife was missing. He made enquiries and eventually discovered her at Runcorn. She declined to return to hins.and said she should remain with the man she loyed.and turned to Gandy. with whom she was walking.—Evidenoe was given to prove that respondent and Gandy bad been living at Widnes toeether.—A. decree nisi with costs against Gandy was granted. A WIGAN SUIT.

In the Divorce Division on Tuesday, a case was heard in which Mrs. Amelia Walmaley. of Haydock, sued for a divorce from her husband, James Walmslc y, a collier, of Wigan, on the ground of cruelay,desertion,and adultery. The case was undefended. The marriage took place in January, 1891, at flaydock. In 1892 respondent left his wife, after assaulting her and pouring paraffin over her clothes and burning them, and threatening to serve her in the same way. It was stated that he had since been living with another woman at Wigan.—& decree nisi was granted.

INTERESTING CEREMONY AT THE OLD ACADEMY, WARRINGTON.

At noon, on Friday,there was an interesting cere- mony at the Old Academy, Bridge Foot, when a number of trees were planted by the Mayor and Mayoress and others. Our readers will perhaps be aware that for many years the Old Warrington Academy has been obscured by what has been known as Marsh's shop. The recent demolition of these premises has brought the Academy to the front once more, and there is every prospect of the building being maintained by the Corporation, The ceremony which took place on Friday was an earnest of this. A poplar tree was planted in front of the building by the Mayor and another by the 1 Mayoress. Ivy was planted by Councillor Bennett, Mr. H. S. Page, author of "The Most Interesting House in Warrington," Mr. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), Mr. J.L. Whittle (town clerk), Mr. Long- din (borough surveyor), and Mrs. J. E. Birtles. 1 Amongst others present were Mr.J.C.Thompson,late master of the School of Art, Mr. T. Birtles, Mr. J. Lunt, Mr. E. J. Ball, and others. At a brief adjourn- ment which was afterwards made, the Mayor expressed the hope that the trees which had been that day planted would live and flourish, and be emblematical of the growth of the town. He be- lieved there was an exceedingly bright future in store for Warrington. Councillor Bennett in pro- posing the health of this Mayor, said he was pecu- liarly gratified that amidst the manifold evidence of progress and prosperity in Warrington they were not forgetting the proud associations of the last century. The Academy with its traditions would be a continual incentive to them to endeavour to make the future of the town in every way worthy of its distinguished past.

The property in dispute is the Feathers' Hotel, with the two cottages and shop adjoining, comprising an area of 636 square yards, eau belonging to Mr. Arthur Wilson. The Umpire : That does not take in any future development at all. Mr. Taylor : That is so, and the question therefore arises what is it fair to assess the taking capabilities of the house at ? It was no use saying as the Corporation valuers had done that the present short tenancy of £64 should be rented on a lease at £BO, and then multiply- 1 ing that rental by 30 years' purchase. They must get at what the takings of the house would be. The opinions of Mr. White and Mr. T. Sutton were that the takings should be £25 per week. Was that too much,or should there be a happy medium between that amount and £l7 10i ? Mr. Tunstall who was the first witness called by the Corporation said in cross-examination, to the amazement of Mr. Bradbury, that the house could not be worked up to its proper taking capacity in the period from September to March, which were the seven months alluded to. That was an important admission on his part. If with an average tenant the full taking capacity could not be realised within seven months, then they ought not to limit the takings to £l7 10s, per week. Was £25 too much to expect ? The witnesses on behalf of the Corporation did not say so. These gentlemen omitted all references to takings because they didn't know the takings when they I made out their valuations. They did not know anything about takings and did not as .ke it an element in their valuations. They merely took the rent. The gentlemen who fixed the takings at £25 per week were those who had a local knowledge and were able to state what the probable takings would be. He was aware that in such cases it was said that those who valued for the claimant made their valuations too high and those on the other side made them toe low, but it was admitted by Mr. Wilson that those who had a local knowledge had a greater advantage. Mr. White had had 30 years experi- ence in dealing with this kind of property, and was he wrong when he said this house should take £25 a week ? They knew £l7 10s. was not a fair criterion. He must also remind them that Mr. Mainwaring who had offices opposite to the hotel also estimated the takings at £25 a week. All the valuations made by the Corporation values were prao- tically identical. There was not that divergence of method or of figures which was generally found when independent valuers made out figures on their own un- bias-ed opinions. They had little local knowledge and they could not do anything else than take the rent being paid, rely upon Mr. Tunstell for information, and make out a valuation identical to what be had made out. There was only one other valuer called by the Corporation and that was Mr. Curran. He said he had valued 40 per cent or 50 per cent of the licensed hews in the to en but when cross-examined it was found that his last valuations had been made some twelve or fourteen years previously. There was precious little value to be at- tached to his evidence. He (Mr. Taylor) thought it was clear from the crose-examination that Mr. Curran hadn't I that experience which he suggested be had. They were therefore drven beak to the evidence of Mr. Tunstall, ' Mr. Wilson and Mr. Kirby. Mr. Tunstall admitted several things which helped the claimant's case very much. Be admitted that there was a large increase of popula- tion, and that that increase had a great effect on the value of property in Bridge-st. That was part of the claimant's case. Mr. Tunstall also admitted that there had been a decrease of one-fourth of the number of licensed houses, and further that there was an amount of through traffic in Bridge-st. In cross-examination be (Mr. Taylor) asked him about property outside this case. Mr. Sutton rather objected, but afterwards Mr. Button was kind enough to ask the arbitrators to look at the value of other places lathe town. If there was anything in it, and there seemed to be, because the Corporation referred them to the value of land between Friar's Gate and the Bridge, he (Mr. Taylor) would ask them to look at Mr.Tunstall's statements with reference to other prop- erties. These properties were Debson's shop in Bridge- et., which had been pulled down, Dell's shop in Sankey- at., and the Millstone Inn in Sankey-st. These had been purchased recently, and the price paid was over £5 per yard in every cue. The Corporation afterwards produced documents showing that they had only paid £2 10s. per yard for land between Friars Gate and the Bridge. But there was one thing they forgot to men- tion and it was this, that they didn't tell the Court that it was the price paid nine or ten years previously,where- as the purchases in Sankey-street, and in other parts of Bridge-street were quite recent. It was not of mush assistance to the Corporation to say that bemuse in 1888 they bought certain land at £2 104 per yard, they must be able to do the same in 1898 He now wished to say something on Mr. Sutton's comments. A great part of Mr. Sutton's speech was taken up ilf referring to the delapidations of the house. Such criticism was wholly Immaterial. No one had suggested that this house was a first-class house. The arbitrators had seen the house for themselves, ane a long argument of that kind was wholly immaterial. Be did net propose to dispute that the house was in a different condition from what it was. 11/r. Sutton then proceeded to say that part of the in- creased trade was due to Mrs. Starkey. He assessed her value to the house at one-fifth, and afterwards said that part of the one-fifth increase might be due to I the destruction of another public-house in the int- rnellete neighbourhood. It showed the value of that kind of observation. Mr. Sutton afterwards commented at greatllength on the peculiar position of Mr Arthur Wit- son,the claimant. His said Mr. Arthur Wilson was not very well off and could not improve the house. He (Mr: Taylor) did not know what the arbitrators thought about it bat such arguments could not affect what Mr. Wilson was entitled to. Prueeding to refer to Mr. Sut- ton's criticisms of Mr. Mainwaring, he said Mr. Sutton was welcome to any crumb of comfort he Gould gain from them. It should be borne in mind that Mr. Main- waling was instructed to value the hotel at a low rate when valuing for the plaintiff in the Chancery proceed- ings, and it mattered very little whether be took into consideration the improvement of Bridge-street or not. Mr. Mainwaring's valuation for the Chancory Court was £5,000 which was very low as was apparent by the fact that Mr. Mainwaring himself offered £B,OOO for it after- wards. Mr. Sutton also said that the claimant's experts were guided by the claini,and that they were bound to make high valuations in consequence of the claim. There had not been a scrap of evidenee to show that Mr. White, Mr. Thomas Sutton or any of the valuers had ever seen the claim sent in to the Corporation. It was purely ima- gination on the part of Mr. Sutton to say that they had seen the claim. It seemed to him (Mr. Taylor) that they might just as well put the boot on the other leg and say that the witnesses for the Corporation had seen their offer, and in consequence had kept their valuations low. In conoln- Mon, Mr. Taylor referred to the claim for the stables, cab business, and the cottages and shop. He pointed Out that the cab business was a fairly good one, more especially in summer, and the situation of the stables made them valuable for the use of farmers and others owning bite the town. With reference to the cottages, be gathered from a remark of the Umpire that that gentleman did not think much of the state of the property. The fact remained that the Medical Officer had not condemned it as insanitary. In assessing the value of the land he asked the arbitrators to bear in mind the price which had been 'Aid by the Corporation for neighbouring lend in Bridge-street and Sankey- street. He had now gone through the %hole of the ease and there were no other comments he wished to make. Do you want me to go asd leak at the pro- perties 1 If lam to take any notice of the evidence in respect to them I must go and. look at them. If I am not to take any notice of them then I shall not go. My feelings are entirely against consider- ing them, unless I leave all. the particulars before me. We all know what properties do fetch sometimes, and It Ii due to certain individual circumstances which makes them valuable. I will go and look if lam to take notice of these figures. Mr. Taylor said be thought it would be wise for the Umpire to do so in this particular case. If the gentle- men who were opposed to him had ignored these pro. parties. and bad not said it was an advantage to have the value of the land between Friar's Gate and the Bridge, he would not have pressed it. THE IRISH NATIONAL LEAGUE. A meeting of the Warrington branch of the above orcanization was held on t•zu ,inv in the Druids' Hall, Sankey-street, the president of the branch presiding. The Chairman said it vvas very gratifying to be able to announce that they had been able to forward to the Dublin Mansion House fund for the Irish distress the sum of .Cl 4 the pro- ceeds of the St. Patrick's Ball held in the Parr Hall, on the 17th March. The committee wished to ex- press their obligations to all who contributed to this successful issue, especially to those who did not think with them, either in politics or religion. The meeting was also addressed by air. Roddy and Mr. Lane, who both expressed themselves highly satisfied with the result of the ball. After the chair- man s address on" 98," he sang" Eileen Alana," for which he received hearty applause. It is expected that Mr. Dillon will soon visit Warrington, and de- liver an address on the present political situation.

SUDDEN DEATH OF M. JAMES BESWICZ. The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Tuesday afternoon of Mr. James Beswick, a retired builder, of Peel-place Church-street, who died suddenly on Saturday afternoon, at the advanced age of 72 years. At ten minutes to four on Saturday Mr. Beswick was walking down Froghall-lane when he fell to the ground, He waS removed to the Infirmary in a passing cart, and en arrival at the institution it was found that life was extinct, death being due,it is believed, to heart dis- ease. No inquest will be held on the body, which was subsequently removed to Peel-place. Deceassd was well-known and deeply respected, an i his sud- den and unexpected end has created widespread sorrow. For nearly fifty years he was an Oddfellow and passed through all the chairs, besides being at one time Provincial Grand Master. He was one of the founders and also a trustee of the Children's Burial Society which was formed in 1868.

APRIL 16'h, 1898.

AN immediate outbreak of America and Spain. war between the United States and Spain now ap- pears to be inevitable. PRESIDENT MCKINLEY has despatched an ultimatum to the Spanish Govern- ment and requires a favourable answer by to-day, otherwise lie will let slip the dogs of war by land and sea. There is absolutely no prospect of the Spanish Government returning a favourable reply. They are, in effect, asked by the Americans to give up the island of Cuba, and they may be relied upon to reply to this modest request with a blank negative, that is to say, if they condescend to make any reply at all. Spain has at the present time a large army in Cuba and that army will stay there until it is expelled. In all probability, therefore, the United States and Spain will be at war against each other within the next twenty-. four hours. Hostilities being taken for granted, people are now speculating as to the results. In this connection there is a fairly unanimous agree- ment that Cuba will in the end be liberated front Spanish control and misgovernment. Look at the question as one may that seems to be a foregone conclusion. It is impossible for Spain to permanently keep its hold of Cuba in the face of American hostility. The future is with the Americans. Spain has made a great figure in European and American his- tory but its best days nave gone. Relatively I speaking, it is now a second or a third rate power. On the other hand tho United States are even yet only on the threshcld of a wonderful development. IThere is positively no comparison between the re- sources, actual and potential, of the States and those of the moribund remnants of the Empire of Charles the Fifth. In one sense, therefore, it may be said that Spain is foredoomed to sooner or Wee lose her American possessions. o,a the other hand the interest of tho moment is centred not so much in the ultimate fate of Cuba, as in the immediate results of the naval and military war-. fare which is about; to commence. Though the States have undoubtedly the staying power which will win in the end, they do not seem to have any marked superiority in the men and material which are necessary to cnrry on a vigorous campaign. It was, we believe, Napoleon himself who said that ) victories were not won by those who had the largest armies but by those who could brine the strongest force to bear upon a given point at 3 given time. When looked at in this way, the proS" poets of the Americans are not quite so promising as well-wishers would like them to be. As a mat-. ter of fact the waging of war is a new business for the people of the United States and one to which they are not much accustomed. It is already reported that they are meeting with unexpected difficulties in getting men anti material together. In this instance the State are under the disadvantage of having to wage an ag" , gressive war. Spain will be quite content with the statu quoante bellum. She is just as much in possesa , sion of Cuba as her rebel subjects will allow her te be, and she has no designs of conquest in connec- tion with the American Continent. Her plan of campaign is, therefore, essentially defensive. It • is summed up in the words of one of her ministers at Madrid who said that if the Yankees wanted Cuba they must come and t4ke it. The authorid ties at Washington have therefore to solve the problem of how to land an army in Cuba and host ; to keep it there when it is landed. The best thing that could happen from their point of vies, would be a big naval battle in which they were victorious, as that would give them the control of the sea between Florida and Cuba. But will the Spaniards think it wise to risk their fortunes in each a contest? On land and in their own Iberian mountains they are adepts at guerilla warfare, ail the French discovered to their cost at the begin.' fling of the present century, and they may think - that a similar kind of warfare at sea will best answer their purpose on the present occasion. In that case they will lay themselves out for harassing the coast towns of their opponent and • for destroying wherever possible American met" chant ships and transports. In this way the war might be prolonged almost indefinitely,to the infinite damage not only of the belligerent natiene themselves but also of all neutral countries which trade with either America and Spain, and espe- cially, of course, of Great Britain. Under the circumstances the most reasonable hope for out.. Odors to indulge in is that, if Spain is really de- termined to fight the matter out, the American may be able to strike a decisive blow either bY sea or land, as that would probably make it clear even to the" proud Don" that a country which tould not repress a rebellion of rustic ereoles 113 Cuba is no match for a nation with a population of seventy millions.

1131 Y influenced, but on the other hand we have the reassuring opinion of fruit merchants doing a , la. rge business with Spain, that it is now too late in the season for it to be much affected. This 171, ew of tho matter seems, on the face of it, to "ye been formulated by a belief that the war flay only last a few weeks. But during the last few d,ys we have seen it stated by American and other experts in the machina- tions of war, that the combat will more likely be one of months, not weeks, and may, (Pita possibly, extend over a twelve-month. Be that as it may, no one can deny with any show of reason, that there is an unsettled feeling in our coramercial circles, resulting from the near and Probable advent of war between the bellicose Yan- kees and the more outwardly pacific, but equally fiery Spaniards.

The agitation for the re-valuation of public- houses for the parposes of local rating is not by anY means confined to Warrington. The question je now occupying the attention of Assessment Com- Mittees through at the country, and amongst the latest in taking action are rating authorities so 'wide apart as itawtenstall in Lancashire and Mile End in East London. The result in both cases has been a substantial increase in the assessments of the public houses. It appears that there are 243 Public•houses in Mile End, and the extent to which tbe3" were undery aluri may be gauged by the Charges which have now been made. One house formerly rate lat £363 has been raised to £624, another from £lO4 to .c2BO. One which stood at the kodest figure of £75 has been increased to £152, another from £320 to £435. It is said that the solicitor employed by the licensed victuallers Ileknowledged that the Assessment Committee had (Wed in a businesslike and impartial fashion. Yet

the reassessment of the licensed houses has resulted In the addition of £2,302 to the proluce of the Mee in Mile End. Most people will agree with the conclusion arrived at by the Manchester "Guardian" which remarks that "If recent cases are typical— which there is no reason to doubt— the general body of ratepayers throughout the country must lose enormously by the undervaluation of public- houses."

It certainly does not speak very much for the in- telligence of the electors of the Borough if so many of them are inflaenced at election times—as the Conservatives would have us believe—by the ap- .pearance of " the yellow coach." And yet great Importance was attached to it at the meeting of Conservativs last week. Sir Gilbert Greenall,Bart, was, of course, in the chair, and his appearance in the Cons,•rvative camp after so long an absence was hailed with great satisfaction. "The yellow coach" Was still on their side, and it was broadly suggested that it should still be allowed to m tke its appear- ance at election times and exert its wondrous charm In favour of the Conservative candidate. From the Ternarks which fall from the lips of one Of the m imbars of the Warrington Junior Conservative Association it appears that the feeling is so strong in favour of lifr. R. Pierpoint, ALP., that, he will be returned at the next election even if he does prefer galivanting in tile Levant in preference to attendance in the House _Of Commons. Mr. Pierpoint may have a personal knowledge of many Places which are receiving Ptablic attention just now, but the speech he de- livered last week does not strike us as very states- raanlike,and it might on the whole be descri bed as au apology for the failures of the Government in its foreign policy.

The assessment of public-houses was again raised at the last meeting of the Warrington Board uf Guardians, and Mr. Sutton (the clerk) re- Peated what he had told us before that in his °Pinion public-houses are assessed at their maxi- -1324123 in Warrington. If Mr. Sutton's remarks aim- applied to the assessments of the two hotels °Wiled by the Warrington Corporation we should be ast one with him for we believe those two hotels are assesed. But on the other hand what is to ;kid of the assessment of many of the tied houses? V 5 have already shown, no once nor twice but nartnY times that there is a great disparity between the assessments of the Corporation hotels where the true rental is ascertainable and those of some tied ilQUaes where the rental is a fictitious one. The very fact that the Assessment Committee increased the assessments of public-houses in Warrington by 41,300 about four years ago shows that they could net have been assessed at their" maximum" prior to that aate. Since the last revision the town has Materially increased in poisulation,and with this in- Freese there is a proportionate increase of business in the public-houses which have slightly decreased in number within that period. The time has un- questionably arrived for a second revision of the as- sessments, and it is to be hoped the Assessment Committee will not shirk their responsibility in the matter.

Labourers wages in Warringion must be ex- tremely low if the statements which are repeatedly Made in various public courts in the town are true. For instance at the Coroner's Court last week, a wornan stated that her husband's wages as a brevi.er's labourer were only 17s. a week. This limited sum was found insufficient to maintain the labourer and his family, and in this case at least it cannot be considered a "living wage." In order to eke out sufficient money for the family's maintain- ence the wife went ou'-, washing, and during her absence one of the children was burnt in such a Shocking manner that it died a few days afterwards tit the Infirmary. We beli,cs there are some 14bourers in Warrington who earn even less than seventeen shillings a week, and considering the large number who are employed in our various in- dustries there must be thousands whose labour is tiot estimated to be worth a week. A strong I Aourers' union is needed.

BOILER, EXPLOSiTiN AT WESTON POINT.

Shortly after five o'clock on Monday niyht, a lend report was heard in the neighbourhood of Weston Point ias fan explosion had occurred. This was unfortu- nately true, but happily it was not attendee with any loss of life although one person was seriously injured, whilst a number of others had narrow escapes. It appears that a man named Rsbertson, a fitter, was engaged looking for in defect it one of the pipes con- nected with the main boiler n the upper eliffitlO room at the Castner Kellner Company's Alkali Works when there was a sudden explosion. Robertson was fearfully scalded about the face, and was knocked into the boiler sells below. The debris caused by the explosion was flung in all direcfions and shattered tie „Ring of the room, and narrowly Oat ip-,1 elm; ng into contact with the other men who were engaged in the room. When Robertson was picked up he was found to be suffering from terrible injuries and was unable to move. Great excitement prevailed in the works and in the immediate neighbourhood. Dr. 31crolugall, et Runcorn, was tele- phoned for, and he in company with Dr. Ormsby hurried to the scene of the explosion. The doctors found the injured man in a precarious condition, and ho was con- veyed to his hose close to. He was severely shaken but no bones were broken. No Satisfactory reason haS yet been given as the cause of the explosion. The accident is all the more sad as it is only recently sines Robertson was united in the bonds of m itrimeny,

--- - - ANOTHER DROWNING MYSTERY AT

--itIINCORN.

INQUEST AND VERDICT.

- Mr. J. R. Worslay, coroner, held an inquest no Moe- day afternoon at Runcorn, into the circumstances attending the death of Edward Carlon who had Nee missing from his home in Granville-street, Runcorn, since March 19th, and whose body was washed up el Saturday out of the Manchester Ship Canal by the steamer Barcelona from Halifax to Manchester, at Old Quay. On the 20ria ult his blood stained cap was found on the banks of the Ship Camel. There was also a large gash ea the forehead.—Jatnes Carlon of Cooper-street, father of deceased said his eon was 52 years of age and was a dock labourer. He saw him last alive in hi, own house four weeks ago. He left to go to his work. Wit- ness afterwards saw him at night on the same day. Ilion Doyle, single woman, living at 25 Mersey-etreet, said she saw the deceased on Saturday night, the 19th ult. He WSJ passing down Mersey-street, a bout ten minutes to eleven o'clock. She could not say whether he was sober or not. Witness did not speak to him, nor did she see anyone with Wm.—Thomas Stanton,labourer if 15,C•oper-street,stated that en the 20th ult,he was go- ing to his work when he found a man's cap on the banks of the Ship Canal.lt was near the boathouse. Re noticed some blood en the ground where the cap was found.— By Superintendent Okell : There was a rope on the ground which was attached to a boat close to. Deceased very prebahly was tripped by this rope. He must have lost a oonsiderable qaantity of blood.—Walter Woothy, bridgeman, of 5, Hartley-street, Runcorn, said he was on duty at the Old Quay,bridge, on Saturday when he saw a body floating, P.C. Gregory deposed to receiving information about a cap being found on the 2011 ult. He went to the , canal and was shown the spot where the cap was picked up. A quantity of blood was on the stones. He picked up 81d. in coppers. He did not notice any footelarks. If there had been a struggle there would here been , foot prints oa the ground. A dredge-boat was tied up near and there was a new rope stretched from the boat to timber on the shore for the purpose of tying the boat up. The rope was tight and three inches above the level of the ground. The blood was six feet from the rope. Elie opinion was that deceased was tripped by the rope and falling forwards after- wards went into the canal. On Saturday afternoon he exatrined the body and found a wound on the fore- head two inches long. The wound was one that could be caused by a man falling forward on the stones. He supposed that deceased when tripeed had his hands in his pockets and so jerked out the money. The road was not a public one. Deceased had no right on that road I at all.—By Superintendent Okell : The night was dark and rough. The upper part of the body was covered with dirt whilst he lower part was quite clean. Hia appeared to have bean stack in the mul.—The jury returned a verdict of "Found drowned."—The Coroner remarked that the case had a sensational aspect about it, but he thought the sensationalism disappeared after the evidence had been given.
