THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, APRIL 22. 1498.

RUNCORN URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

ANNUAL MEETING. MR. S. TAYLOR RE-ELECTED CHAIRMAN.

The annual meeting of the Runcorn Urban District Council was held on Tuesday evening, in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall, Runcorn. There were present Messrs. S. Taylor, J.P., J. Wilson, H. J. Grice, E. Gatlev, W. Collier, G. El. Kirkham, J.Jonee, J. Prit- chard, G. Hewitt, J. W. Lightburn, J.Dean,D.Risbrown, M.J. Sweetman, J. Hanley; F. A. Lake (law clerk), E. Marshall (secretary),and J. Wilding (surveyor.) Mr. Wiswall was votel to the chair pre tent. The first business of the meeting was the eiection of chairman for the ensuing year. Mr. Wilson moved that Mr. S. Taylor be re-elected chairman of the Urban Council for the ensuing year. Mr. Taylor was a gentleman who had served the Coon- cil and the ratepayers well, and he was sure that if his proposition was carried it would give general satisfac- tion to the people. Tale past year had been a noted one for the chairman of the Council, and he considered that they could not de a more graceful and kindly act than , to ask Mr. Taylor to accept office for another year. ' Mr.Lightburn seconded the proposition: He thought I it would be no surprise to the pablio to know Mr. Taylor had been re-appointed. The previous year bad been an arduous one and Mr. Taylor had filled it with marked ability. They had every reason to depart from their good custom of appointing a new chairman year by year Mr. Grice said he was very much gratified as a mem- ber of the Council to see the Authority had done this most graceful act. Mr. Taylor's position during the year had been a very unique one, and it had been very uniquely filled. They could not have improved their choice for the last year and he (Mr. Grice) was certain they could not have found another gentlem ea in Lag- land who could have filled the poet so well. The reeolution we carried unanimously. Mr. Wiewall then vacated the chair and said he had much oleagure in doing so. He announced that Mr. Taylor. was re-elected chairman by the unanimous vote of the Council. He was sure that he had never made such au announcement with more pleasure and satisfac- tion then he did this. Mr. Taylor then took the chair. He thanked them most sincerely for placing him in the position they had done that night for another year. He felt most grateful to the members of the Council for the unanimous sup- port they had given him during the past year. It had made his duties very light, most agreeable, and most comfortable, and he felt equally grateful to them and hoped that in the coming year he would have the same support because a chairman could do very little unless he had the support of these over whom he presided. I Referring to the new rate Mr. Taylor said that lie mete- tog the estimates for the coming year they had unfor- tunately to put on an increase of 3d.ln the £ on the rates. They all felt volved and reluctant to have to do it, but as they knew they ought to have done it twelve months ago or more. He believed every councillor felt as he felt himself that they were hopeful of having what better better times during the past year, and that they might go through without advancing the rate, but this year they had had to face it. Me considered the cloud of depression was now passing over Runcorn. (Hear, hear.) 3 Works were being extended and new ones were being established. He felt hopeful and he was almost sure that the state of things would be vastly changed this time next year. (Hear, hear.) MR. 7', W. LIGHTEORN RE-BLECTED VICE-CHAIRMAN. The Chairman moved taat Mr. Lightbern be the vice- chairman for the ensuing year. When it had been hie- p )asible for him (Mr. Taylor) to attend—he thought he had been absent on two occasions during the year when he was away from home—Mr. Lightburn was always to take his place. fie was sure that he could not better co-worker than Mr. Lightburn.—Mr. Jones ehweacivloleinn a dged the proposition.—Mr. Wiswall supported the motion and considered that the vice-chairmanship could not be in better hands than Mr. Lightburn next to him- self (the chairman). The proposition was unanimously cerrie 1. Mr. Liehthurn said that at all times he had endeavoured to „iv I the chairman all the support he possibly could,and he would continue to do for the next twelve months. Ho thought they had re +son to expect a better state of things in the cumin,/ 0 year. The Council had done much to develop trade in the place even more than the public thought,and they hoped they would continue that p:licy during the present year.

TSB PORM&TION OP VIE COMMITTEES.HELTHD DISCISSION.

Mr. Wiswall proposed chat in the formation of the committees they revert to the old prae;tice, namely, in- stead of having all the members of the Council on the committees to have eight or nine on each committee. The present mode of procedure made their monthly meetings formal, and it had been said that their officials governed the town. Well, that was no reflection on the officials bet it was on them.— dr. Lightburn said that by making the whole beard me:lll),re of a committee it gave some of the new members experience or the council work, but that had now passed away. The great ad- vantage of a small committee was that they had public opinion outside that committee. No one was to blame outside the committee, and if the committee had ex- ceeded its estimates the otuer members of the council could say to the chairman of that particular committee "you have exceeded your estimates,give us your reason." He had pleasure in seconding the motion.—Mr. Dean moved an amendment that they keep to the arrange- ment they had been working on for the last three or four years. The arguments advanced in favour of the change were very poor. lie challenged the statement that any committee had exceeded their esti- matee. He did not believe they had.—Mr. Wilaon : I do not thoroughly understand the ancient practice. I have been connected with the Council for seine time but I know of no other arrangement than the present one.— Mr. Wiswall said whether ancient or not it was &leis- factory to him. The old arrangement used to be that the Highways and Buildings Committee Was corn- posed of all the members of the Council. The Health Committee had no limit, and the number varied from six to twelve. If his propesition was carried any coun- cillor could refuse to sit on a committee, but by the pre- sent arrangement he could not. He was put on a com- mittee whether be liked it or not. That was what he objected to all along. They had no right to put a mem- ber on a committee against his will.—Mr. Grine secon- ded the amendment. He remembered that when they acted en ancient hues as Mr. Wilson bad termed the old arrangement they could hardly get a quorum. He did not see that a member failing to attend a Committee, meeting was barred from taking an antagonistic view of thr subject at the Council meeting. The present ar- rangerstent saved time and a lot of trouble, and they got through the work more agreeably. By every member , being on a committee they could not shirk their dutiee ' —Mr. Wiswall said the proposer of the amendment had remarked that no committee had exceeded' its estimates. That Might certainly be true, but they had certainly not reduced them. The actual expenditure was the important matter, and let them get rid of this over-draft at the bank, which they had year by year. (Hear, bear.) In his opinion the present system of committees did net teed to ecoeolery.— Mr. Pritchard said the Urban District Council was con- stituted three or four years ago. The rate at that time was 21. 9d. in the Z. Now they were levying a rate of 3s. 3d. in the £, and according to the estimates it would take a 3e. 6d. rate to clear them without any deficiency at the close of the ensuing twelve months. They saw that in the four years since this authority was con- stituted there had been an increase Of ed. in the £ upon tbe rates. He was prepared to say that a portion of that might be in consequence of empty houses. (Hear, hear.) Now, he was of opinion that had they had email cone- ' mittees to watch this expenditure as they had in pre- vious years before that authority was established, the rate would not stand as it did at present. —Mr. Wilson said he did nc t agree with the arguments put forth by Mr. Pritchard- He could not possibly at- tend every committee meeting, but there were certain committees he attended and did his best. On any subject that had not Met his views in committee he had always expressed himself as well as he was able to do, and if he lost in the fight then when the Council meeting came round he loyally stood by the majority. Mr. Pritchard had stated that if the small committees were in opera- tion they could eertainly put their finger on the expen- dieure and keep it down, but why couldn't a big one do it? What was the reason why a big committee could not do it P—Mr. Liehtburn : They say everybody's business is nobody's.—Mr. Wilson : But I say it is yours—Mr. Dean : Formerly a id. in the £ realised Ent, and now a lci. rate is £lBo.—Mr. Marshall : £l9s.—Mr, Dean: The old adage is—the biggest number of councillor; the more wisdom.—Mr. Collier thought that Mr. Pritchard had really smashed up the case al- together. Widnes and Liverpool had small committees and they had increased rates. RUNCORN SEAMEN'S INSTITUTE.

SALE OF WORN.

On Wednesday afternoon in the Seamen's Institute, Runcorn, Mrs. W. J. Crossley of Altrineham, opened a three days' sale of work. The object of the sale is to raise funds to clear off the debt of £l5O upon the above named institute. _ _ _

Amongst those present were Mrs. W. J. Crossley, of Iltrincham,the Rev. Mr. Lambert, chaplain to the Sea- men's Inatitute, Mr. J. K. Digges, hon. see.. Mr. H. A. Cooke, Mr. Newton, Mr. R. Abel, Captain Heasley, and Mr. W. Shaw (missionary). The stalls were well filled with articles of almost every description. The Rev. Mr. Lambert said it was his pleasing duty to call upon one who had been along with her husband a strong support to the Mersey Mission to Seamen' to wish God speed to this effort they were making to free the Runcorn Institute of all the debt that remained upon the land and buildings. Mrs. Crossley would declare the day's proceedings open in a few minutes. but before doing so, perhaps she would allow him to congratulate all the workers on having got such a tine array of stalls. It was a source of satisfaction that they had organised this sale of work in a spirit of true independence, so that they would be able to say that they had never called on the general fund. They knew the cause of the small attendance that, afternoon, namely, an event which had cast a shado* over many homes in the town. Be referred to the death of Mr. J. E. Langshaw. Mr. Langshaw had helped at their last' bazaar, and had shown a very great interest in the present sale of werk. It seemed almost a coincidence in this branch where they had distinguished themselves by providing a sailors grave and monument in order to bury sailors who had been drowned about Runcorn, and who were far away from any friends who were able to pay the burial ex- penses, that 80 many of the pnople who would have supported them that afternoon were to be found near that monument in the cemetery. He considered that there had been increieingfellowship in tois branch of the Mersey Mission all along the line ever since Mr. Shaw came 23 years ago. He had much pleasure in calling on Mrs. Crossley to declare the sale of work open. (Ap- plause). Mts. Crossley said it was a great pleasure to her to be there that afternoon. It ,vas the second Sale of Work she had attended since she returned to England from a very long holidiy. Sales of work made her feel proud, particularly after her sojourn in Esypt. Ia Egypt women were not speken of as they were in England. The Egyptians thought they were not fit to work and abso- lutely ignored them. Bat when they came to sales of work they found out what a woman could do. (Laugh- ter.) When she was on the Nile and nearing her time to return home, her man-servant wanted to come with her. She asked him what he would do and he said "clean for you and look after your room." (Laughter.) She told him that women did that work in England. This answer he treated with contempt. The Egyptians considered that women never did anything; that was right, and al- ways everything that was wrong. She was glad to know that the efforts which were being made to fres that institute from debt were being don by women. It was their duty to do what they could to make the world better than they tound it. DEA.Ta OF MR. J. E. LANGSII.A.W, OF

RIINCO RN.

It is our sad duty to record the Oise% of Mr. James E. Langshaw, which took place at his residence lee. Church Place, Runcorn, early on Sunday inorning,at the early age of 33 years. About a month ago Mr. Lang- shaw was one of the coneregation that attended the Confirmation service at the Parish Church. During the service a lady fainted and he left the church without his hat and went across to his house to gat some water. The night was very cold, and when he returned to the church tie was heard to complain that be felt very chilly. He improved a little, but he did not tako any serious notice of himself until about a fortnight ago. lie visited Oldham about three weeks ago and watched the match, Oldham v. Runcorn. That was tti last occasion on which he was seen out of doors. The ehin which had evidently not left him entirely, took a srork2er hold of its victim,and developed into an acute form of influenza. Dr. McLennan had been assiduous in his at,cedance and all that medical skill could do to pro:oug life was brought to bear on the patient, but de ,pite all this he died from pneumonia at the time stated. sdr. Langshaw was well-known almost all over the cola:re, where his business as an employo of the Salt Union, and as secre- tary to the Runcorn Football Club took him. He had held the position of secretary to the Runcorn Football organisation nearly twenty years and bad watched its developments from a fourth-rate position to that of one of the best football clubs in the country under the Rigby code. Before the formation of the Northern Ragbr Union, Mr. Langshaw was a member of the Cheshire County Committee. On seceding to tho Northern Union. Runcorn carried off the new union's first challenge cup, and were distinguished as baing proud holders of the championship. Mr. Langthaw's knowledge was recog- nised under the new regime by his appointment on the General Committee and the Lancashire Committee, and he was one of the select few appointed to consider ail draft a scheme of professionalism. Mr. Ltueshaw also filled the post of hon. secretary and treasurer to the Cheshire County Football Union,and was treasurer te the Runcorn Tontine Society the deceased gentleman had also been proosieently identified with the Runcorn Parish IChurch, and had held the position of sideoman there for ' many years. lee had also been connectsd with the Mersey Mission to Seamen, Runcorn Branch, and had done excellent service to the cause. He was a member of the committee appointed to make arrangements for the Sale of Work which was opened on Wednesday in the Seamen's Institute. The sad event cast a gloom over the day's proceedings, and reference was made to de- ceased by the Rev. Mr. Lambert, chaplain to the mis- sion. His death in the prime of life is deeply regretted by the whole town.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

TEM WHISKEY WAS Too Sriaoio. At the Warrington Borough Court on Friday morning Joseph Caslin, Boar's Head Inn,was charged with having on the 17th March sold to Ralph Norman, a quantity of whiskey which was adulterated with 50 per cent. mere water than was allowed.—He pleaded guilty.—The Town Clerk prosecuted and said the whiskey was found to contain 50 per cent, of water in excess of what was allowed. The Act allowed 25 degrees of water under proof, and this whiskey contained 30 degrees.—Defen- dant said be had (Illy been 6 months in the trade and was ignorant of the law concerning whiskey. He tasted tt, and thinking it was too strong, he plat some water in —The Clerk : Is not the whiskey delivered to ycu ready adulterated P—Defendant : I knew it was but I thought it could be reduced a ti,t naore.—A fine of 10a. including costs was imposed. A M tr.x-DiaLan Strmaroamax Before the magistrates at the Warrington Police Court on Friday,Mary Barnes, 14,Golborne-st., was summoned for having on the 25th March sold a quart of inilk,which was adulterated with water.—The Town Clerk prose- cuted, and Mr. Draper (Messrs R. Davies and Co.) de- tended.—The Town Clerk said defendant was aharged under Section 5 of the Food and Drugs Act. The offence was committed on the 25th March on which data Intim- ter Norman employed a boy to buy a quart of milk from defendant's daughter, who was retailing it in Beath Side. The boy paid 34. for the milk, which was divided Into three parts,exarained and certified by the Medical Ofßcer of Health to contain 12 per cent of water.—lnspector Norman, and the boy referred to, eorreborated.—Mr. Draper, for the defence. mentioned that his client had been in busi- ness as a milk dealer for nearly 20 rears, and had never had a complaint against her. She got her milk from a Cheshire farmer of high reputation, and on the day is question she receivei a tankard from him. This tankard was taken out at once, and the milk retailed Irons it. Dgenriant knew nothing about the state of the I milk. He believed that the cause of the a iulteration was that by an oversight, the water put in the tankard at the farm, to cool it, had been overlooked, and the milk poured in on top of it. The To en Clerk said he had reason to believe that this was the Cale.—Defendant was merely ordered to pay costs.

THE POPULA.R LECTURES AT PA.RE

HALL.

MEETING OF THE COMMITTEE.

In the Museum Lecture Room on Monday even- ing a meeting of the General Lecture Committee which arranged the recent pioneer lectures was held Councillor F. W. Monks presided, and there was a fair attendance.

===== 0002601_18980422_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
THE REPORT AND BLI.A.NCI SHEET.

The report of the committee on the last course of lectures which appeared in last week's "Examiner" was submitted.—The Chairman said that, taken all I round, the attendance was fairly good. The only room for disappointment was the fact that more front seats were not sold at 2.. 61. If they had sold a larger number they would have had more than sufficient to wipe out the adverse balance of .£3 101. 61.—Mr. John Dewhurst said the great drawback was the cold state of the ball. (Hear, hear.) Ile heard many complaints; the cold was so dreadful that many people would not go to the lectures a second time.—Mr. Tbotnas May said he could confirm what the last speaker had said. Re- peated complaints were made to him. At the same tirce,the Chairman and others were to be congratu- lated on the quality of the lectures,the attendance, and the very small deficiency.—Mr.Malkin observed that many people stayed away on account of the cold.—Mr. Dewhurst : One of the lecturers de- scribed it as " a small tempest on the platform."— The Chairman: It was no fault of ours. I did all I could to get the room right. The ball is always cold fatless it is packed.—After some further dis- cussion on the same subject, the report and state- ment of accounts were adopted.

THE ZILLA.H SHIPPING COMPANY.

ANOTHER NEW STEAMER.

It is only a very few years since the Zillah Ship- ping Company was formed by a number of gentle- men chiefly belonging to Warrington and Widnes, and yet during that short period its progress has been remarkable. Commencing with one vessel.the "Zillah," from which the company takes its name, under the able guidance of Mr. W. A. Savage, ot Warrington, the company has had an unchequered career of prosperity, and it has now four vessels afloat, and another, it may be added, is in course of building. The most recent addition to the steamers of the company is the "Sarah Brough" which die charged her first cargo at Widnes on Tuesday. Her arrival was witnessed by a large number of Widnes I people, and as she lay in the West Bank dock with flags floating gaily in the breeze, she presented a striking spectacle. The vessel is admitted to be one of the finest coasters trading with this part of the country. Measuring 137 ft. 6in, in length, 23ft. beam, and 10ft. 6in, in depth, her displacement is spo cons. Her carrying capacity is 350 tons exclu-n sive of 20 tqns bunker acaommodatien. She is to per cent. over Lloyd's requirements in all depart- ments, and the opinion formed of her may be judged by the fact that she is marked " A. I. plus" at Lloyd's, which is the highest position given. Sae is provided with four collision water-tight bulkheads. 2 forward and 2 aft, anti her water ballast capacity is between 60 and 70 tons. A fine saloon heautifully fitted up with maple and oak and handsomely furnished is provided, and there are also a Master's room and Chief Engineer's room amidships. There is further accommodation aft for two engineers, the second engineer, and firemen, and in the forecastle for six of a crew. The machinery throughout is the best that engineering skill can devise. The cylinders of the envine are 17X35 inches, and work It a pressure of 130 pounds to the square inch. She is 24 inch stroke, and about 500 horse power. She has also a condenger and seamless boilers.. Her speed is II knots per hour which is exceedingly i fast for !a coasting vessel. The vessel s also equipped with a steam capstan and steam windlass, and connected with the anchor there is a Clarke- Chapman's patent steam windlass. The Captain's bridge is furnished with first class nautical appara- tus. The Ann Ship Building Company, of Troon, Ayrshire, have built the vessel, and the engines have been pat in by Messrs. Ross, and Duncan, of Glasgow. She was launched in January and her trial trip took place on Easter Monday when a num- ber of Warrington and Widnes gentlemen were on board, and enjoyed a trip round the Isle of Arran. On Saturday last she left Troon for Liver- pool and arrived there the lame night. Captain W. A. Savage directed the vessel to Fleetwood on Monday,and on Tuesday he returned with her to Widnes with a cargo of 320 tons of rock salt. Her performances up to the present have given entire satisfaction, and she will ply chiefly between Lancashire and North Wales and Ireland. Captain Savage who is the founder of the company will be in charge of the vessel. The other steamers in the possession of the company are the " Priscilla," captain, Daniel Atherton ; " Merlin, Captain, Samuel Summerfleld ; " Zillah," captain, William Hulee. At the present time a sailing yes- " sel is being built at Connah's Quay for the company. and it is expected this will be launched early in May. She will be called the "Winifred."

THE TAXATION OF LAND VALUES By JOSEPH HYDEB, General Secretary to the Land N4tionalis..wu Society.

ARTICLE XXII.

' We havejust been driving along your magnifleent Elta- bankment. but at whose expense was that great, permanent and stable improvement made I. Instead of being made, as it should have been, mainly at the expense of the perman- ent proprietary interests, it was charged, every hitting of it upon the occupants, either upon the wages of the labouring man ia fuel necessary for his family, or upon the trade and industry and enter prise which belong of necessity to a vast, metropolis like this.—Ma. GLADSTONE at the Memorial Hall, London, on July 29th, 1837. The laud around these populous centres Is rated not in re- gard to its prospective value, which as you all know, is de- rived entirely from tics proximity of phases where enterprise and activity are, while the owners of the surrounding land where asleep in theit beds —adding every day, every hour to Its value —Mn. JOHN' MORLEY at F.dfar on Monday, October 4th, 18.7 C .

No survey of the land quecion would be complete which did not take account of the movement for the taxation of land values. It is a movement which has attained considerable strength of recent years, and is destined to make its mirk upon the statute Book. It includes men of very divergent views. The principle of taxing land has found supporters in the ranks of both the great parties in the State. Practically every Liberal to committed to it. Even some Conservatives believe in it, I is endorsed by the Socialists. A little time ago an imp trtant conference upsu it was held at Bradford, and it was reported that over 200 a,sessing bodies in England and Seotland had passed resolutions in its favour. More recently still the Battersea vestry convene a conference of the rating authorities in London, when it was supported by an overwhelming majority of the delegates present. Al the last municipal elections in Glrsgow a majority of the waole number of candidates were in favour of it. At the recent election of the Lon- don County Council every Progressive candidate was pledged to it. And it was one of the items of the New- castle Programme.

===== 0002601_18980422_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
TRR UNFAIR RIBMPTION OF LANDLORDS

from their t-a-xation is a grievance which the main body of the taxpayers have long borne uncomplainingly. But at length their silence is being displaced by the unmistakable grumbling and agitation which ueually precede important chan ;es. Nor can this be wondered at. For the burden of taxation tends every year to become heavier. And fast though the wealth. producing power of the country increases, yet the national taxes and loeal rates increase faster srill. Under ovary heading the cost of government goes up by leaps and hounds, and no one can say where it ia possible .to effectively reduce it. There are so many measures of , public improvement which await accomplishment that we must be prepared for a further increase in public expenditure rather than any decrease. Therefore the only way of lighteniog the burden of taxation is to fini a new source of revenue. Such a new sourze of revenue lies at hand is the shape of the value of land which has hitherto contributPd far less than personaliiy to the cost of government. The fallowing table will show at a glands how enormously the demands upon the public exchequer have grown within the past thirty years : LOCAL TAXATION. 1867. 1892. Ragland and Wales £23 967,000 .„. £47.439,559 Scotland Ireland

Total £29 257,000 £57,397,737 IMPERIAL TAXATION, 1874. 1884. 1895. £74,921,873 £88,043,110 £101,974,000 In these days of severe business Competition this INCREASING LOAD Or TAXATION Is a merlons matter to large numbers of people. Thir- teen years ago a great rating authority, the Vestry of St. Pancras, sent a petition to Parliament on this matter. They showed that they represented a population of 250,000 and a rateable value of £1,513,910. They com- plained that they experienced a greater difficulty from year to year in raising rates from persons of limited means owing ,to the increasing public expenditure of Metropolitan authorities. During the last half-year they had had to summon 5,895 persons before the magis- trates for the non-payment or rates. They showed that tt,e .rates at present fail most heavily upon the retail trading and working classes, and those who benefit most largely by the increased value of land in large towns are exempt from contributions towards local taxation, notwithstanding the fact that such taxation is largely expended in Increasing the value of the land, especially where street and other public improvements have been made at the cost of the ratepayers. They concluded their petition with the prayer that the owners of land might be rated. In the same year (1885) the Royal Commission on the Housing of the Poor recom- mended a similar reform. They called attention to the fact that at present land available for building in the neighbourhood ef our populous centres. though its capital value is very treat, is probably producing a I smaller yearly return until it is let for building. The owners of this land are rated, not in relation to the real value butte the actual annual income. They can thus afford to keep their land out of the market, and to part with only small quantities, so as to raise the prica lbeyond the natural ntnnopsly price which the land would command by its ad eaatages of position. Mean- tints, the general expenditare of the town on Improve- ments is inereasing the value if their property. If this land were rated at, say, 4 per cent, on its selling value, the owners would have a more direct incentive to par with it te those who are desirous of building, and

A TWOFOLD ADVANTAGE would result to the community. First, all the valuable property would contribute to the rates, and thus the the burden on the occupiers would be diminished by the Increaae in the rateable property. Secondly,tho owners of the building land would be breed to offer their land ter sale, and thus their competition with one another weou!d brine down the price of building land, and so diminish the tax in the shape of ground rent, or pries paid for lead, which is now levied on urban enterprise by the adjacent landowners ; a tax, be it remembered, which is no recompense for any industry or expenditure on their part, but is the natural result of the industry aid activity of the townspeople themselves. Having regard to all these considerations, the Royal Commis- sioners therefore urged that "these matters should be Included in legislation when the law of rating comes to be dealt with by Parliament." Yet, simple and just though that reform is, it has hitherto been withheld. The effects ef the landlord legislation of the past are thus perpetuated through the supineness of landlese tax- payers, who, like the camel, kneel down while they are being loaded. And meantime the day of "the last straw" most unaecountably tarries.

===== 0002601_18980422_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
THE USN FOE LONDON.

Take the case for London. Before the Town Holdings Committee evidence was given by the late William Saunders to the effect that the total value of property for rating purposes throughout the Metropolitan area was as follows : Capital value of London land £4ls 000,000 Capital value of London buluilnos £212,030,000 The owneis of the £418,001,000 for land pay no rates, and the only taxation to which they are subjected is the Income tax and a nominal land tax, the total of both being less than half a million a year. Houses are charged with rates which average (say) Si. 64. in the £, not only apon the annual value of the buildings, but SIM upen the annual value of the land. In addition to the rates they pay house duty and income tax. In 1863 London paid the rate collector £2,719,272. In 1890 this had in- creased to £7,244,067. And this amount is much greater now. So the Londen case stands thus : Land worth £418.000,000 oars £500.000 In rates. Buildings worth £212.000000 pay £9.0430.000 in rates. Of course what applies to Landon applies to ovary other town. The figures will be smaller in other cases, but the inequality and the injustice will be about the same. And as if the urban ratepayer did not have enough to complain of already, he has been lately saddled, by the iniquituous Agricultural Rating Relief Act of the present Government,with the weight of paying an extra £2,000,000 a year, ostensibly for the relief of agricul- tural tenants, but in reality for the benefit of agricul- tural landlords. Thus were the landlords revenged for Sir William Harcourt's increase in the estate duties,and the urban voters rewarded for their loyalty to their masters at the 3.895 election. Verily a setting back of the clock. There remains, however, consolation in the knowledge that it is only for a time, if the people can by any chance be made alive to their own interest.,

CAN THR LANDLORDS SHIFT THE TAX? It is necessary here to refer to an important question, about which a furious controversy has raved, "Can the landlrds shift a tax on land values back upon the ten- ant ?" If he can, then wherein will the tenant he bane- ptred by the proposed change P Adam Smith, Ricardo Mill, Rogers, and in fact all the economists, agree the a tax on rent stops where it falls. Says John Stuar Mill A tax on rent falls wholly on the landlord. There are no means by which he can shift the burden upon anyone else." This argument always pre-supposes that other things remain unchanged. It is quite true that, generally speaking, a landlord gets as much for his land as he possibly can. He could not get more tor it mer.dy because his household expenses had increased. Whether er not he can shift the tax will be determined solely by the way in which it is to be @trent. If it be thrown into the sea, or spent on war, or employed for sorno new purpose, then the landlord cannot recoup himself. But if it be employed to effect a corresponding remission of taxes which the occupier sow pays, then the landlord will be able to sweep into his pocket as

increased rent a part at least of the benefit which the tenant would otherwise wholly receive in reduced taxa- tion. All authorities are agreed that landlords gain in higher rent what the tenants ought to gain in lower tax- ation. I remains to he seen how far this tendency will be counterbalanced by the taxation of land values. Henry George in " Progress and Poverty," Bo k VI., chapter i., says : ‘.

- "A reduction in the amount taken from the aggregate produce of a cimmunity by taxation would simply c. equivalent to an increase in the power of net produ f tion. It would,in effect,add to the productive power o labour, just as do the increasing density of population and improvement in the arts. And as the advantage in the one case goes,and must go. to the owners of land in increaeed rent, so would the advantage in the other." At any rate, the experiment must be tried,as it would be a valuable means of cutting down monopoly rents which now obtain because of land being withheld by rich @penmen or speculators. And further,a tax upon land values is the ideal means of raising revenue for new public expenditurs,thus at least preventing any further taxation of tenants. As a fiscal reform, therefore, I support it. But, both for its ineffieiency as well as for its inequity,l am entirely opposed to the policy known as "taxing landlords Out."

SAILING MATCHES. A race in connection with the Warrington Yacht Club, took place on Saturday. It was very suitable weather for the occasion. Four cempetitors en- tered :—Mr. Harry Turner's " Im n" ; Mr. Jefferson's "Penitent ;" Mr. Thomas Whittle's " Saint .." and Mr. Chapell's " Sinner" The course was from a point near Hutching'a Tannery to Dobbie's Lock twice round, and meiv.ured four miles, The first boat over the line was the " Penitent," the "Imp, "Sinner," and " Saint." following Nine ascends only separated the four boats. The "Sinner," broke her rudder iron early in the race and had to retire. The '• Penitent," was the first, to arrive, haying covered the course in one hour 29 minutes. "Imp" followed, her time being one hour 29 minutes, 58 seconds. And "Saint' last in one hour, 36 minutes 10 rieconds. The race counts three points to Mr. Jefferson's " Penitent." On Wednesday evening an operetta entitled "The Magic Chain " was Riven at the Latchford Parish Room, in aid of Christ Church Parish sehools,by the Misses Broadbent. Brockman, and Meekly, and members of the Christ Church Day schools and choir It was very well attended. The scenery was effective and quite in keeping. The children who took part in th. operetta, were highly trained, and took their parts both individually and collectively in a most efficient manner. The young ladies who took the principal characters in the cast, showed ability and talent in their representations. The plot may be briefiy described as follows :The Princess (as in the usual fairy story), is being wooed by tb. Prince. The witch casts a spell over the PrineeSe despite the endeavour. of the Fairy Queen and Fairies. Eventually the Fairies break the spell by the aid of their" Magic Chain," and the Prince and Princess are made happy. The different songs by the ladies were well received. Mrs. Adams made a very effective and sympathetic accompanist. The performance was repeated on Thursday evening. 'INDEPENDENT ORDER 0? MEETINGS OF SPIRITUALISTS.

The usual meetings of Spiritualists were held on Sunday and Monday in the Academy-street Temper- ance Hall. when Mrs.L. A. Peters,of Manchester was the speaker. She gave good addresses at both meetings on Sunday, the subject of that in the evening being "Immortality," suggested by a mem- ber of the audience. She was also successful in clairvoyance. On Monday evening, after the usual opening services and the reading of a ptem by Sir Edwin Arnold, Mrs. Paters occupied the time with clairvoyance and psychometry.

GOOD TBMIPLARY. The usual weekly session of the Temperance Mission Lodge of the 1.0 G.T. was held in the Temperance Hall, Academy-street, on Wednesday. There was a good attendance. After the usual business had been traneacted Bro. Price moved that a vote of condolence be sent to Sister J. B. Colling (Birmingham) on the sudden death of Bro. J. B. Collings, editor of the "Watchword." Bro. O'Brien seconded and the motion was carried unanimously. Bro. Roberts also spoke a few words on the aubject Brother Roberts gave a report of the Grand Lodge session which was held at Plymouth last week. Bro. O'Brien spoke on the report which was ac- cepted. Bro. J. Sheath gave • report of the No. 1 Convention session which was held at Leigh on Saturday. He notified that a committee was to be formed to consider the advisability of having a Con- vention Guide. Bres. Mortimore, O'Brien. Sheath, and Roberts spoke on the question. Bro. Mortimere wet appointed to act for the lodge on the commit- tee. Bro. Roberts closed the loge at 9 40.

===== 0002601_18980422_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WARRINGTON VOLUNTEERS.

A tactical schen;TOr—th7-cYcliat Section of the Volunteers was carried out on Saturday. Captain List,

_ B. Fairclough commanded Blue, and Second —,eu- tenant G. E. Crosfield Red. Red were sent to Great Budworth Church, and Blue ta Daresbury, with orders not to leave those places before 3.30 p.m. The Blue force was only double the strength of Red, thus making it almost impossible for Blue to stop them getting through under the rules laid down. Blue divided his force into five patrols, No. 1. Daresbury ; 2, Hatton; 3 and 4, Stretton ; 5, Old Farm near Appleton Thorn. Both officers showed by their action that they had made a careful study of the ground. Blue posted his patrols at the most crucial points and in good positions. Red expect- ing that his flanks would he most strongly guarded chose Hatton as the point to get through Blue, which, besides being the centre of the area, gave him the shortest ride from Budworth, and he there had also the advantage of alternative roads to War- rington if repulsed. The Red despatch was delivered at Wilderspool Brewery at 4.30 p.m., thus giving Red the victory. Red was entrusted with three despatches, two false to mislead enemy and one genuine from Officer Commanding Red Force at Warrington. The two false ones were marked "Not to be torn up if captured," while the genuine one was to be destroyed if in danger of capture. Red commander sent the two false ones in charge of the Cyclist Sergeant (Millett) and one cyclist, telling them to avoid capture, but not to destroy the despatches, which were eventually Captured by No. 3 and 4 Blue patrols at Stratton. The Red. com- mander, who had rightly divined the nature of the despatches, took the genuine one himself with an escort of two cyclists, choosing Hatton as the but point to break through Blue lines; he was there encouattred by Blue Patrol No. 2, but got through, as he bad a superior force under the rules. A fair proportion of the cyclists turned out, and looked smart with their Lee-Metford rifles slung across the back, and the scheme was entered into with much spirit by all concerned, Captain Appleton and Second Lieutenant Bernhard acted as umpires, and Colour-Sergeant WilcoCk as their orderly.

MOBE UNSOUND MEAT AT WARRIN6TON.

TWO BUTCH eRS FINED • SEVERE PENALTY. On Friday morning at the Warrington Police Court before the Mayor (chairman), Councillor Roberts and Councillor Smethurst,Thomas Woolley, 172, Liverpool road,was charged with exposing lor sale on March 31st, five pieces of beef weighing 43i lbs, which were unsound and unfit for human food. —Woolley was unable to appear through illness. The Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whttle), prosecuted on behalf of the Corporation, and said that defendant was charged under section 116 ot the Public Health Act of 1875. On the 31st M acti he expo ied for sale certain ' pieces of meat, which were seized by Inspector Norman, and certified to be unfit for human food. Defendant carried on business at 172, Liverpool-road, and on the 31st March,lnspector Norman was passing and oeserved these pieces of meat exposed in the usual way. Ile seized them and conveyed them to the Town Hall,where Dr.Gornall certified them to be unfit for food and on the order of the magistrates, the beef was destroyed. Defendant was liable to a penalty of £2O for each piece of meat, and the Town Clerk reminded the Bench that the onus of proving that the meat was not intended for sale rested with defendant. There was another case hinging upon this and he should like the bench to hear it before giving their decision. Inspector Norman, in corroborating the Town Clerk, said there were altogether five pieces weighing 43S2ebs, The meat was wet and soft, and a round ot beet on the counter, when he took the skewers out, swabbed about as if it was a mud-bank. About seven p.m. the same day, Woolley called at his office and asked to see the meat. He examined it and said it smelled pretty sweet. Witness said " I should think your nose has gone if you thick that is sweet." Defendant said "Do you purpose summoning me in this case," and was told that the matter would be laid before the committee. Defendant then said " If that is the case then there is nothing but the b— gaol for me this time. I have not seen this meat before to-night. I am working in Liver- pool and when I came off the train I met Bradbury outside the station,and be asked use if I could do with a good round of bed and I told him I could do with some fur morning and arranged with him to send it up. I have not seen it until now." Witness had no reason to doubt that he bad not seen it. Dr. Gornall (meaical officer of health), deposed to x-:e amining the meat and finding it unfit for human food. Mr. Fergusson, veterinary Burgeon, agreed with all , that Dr. Gornall said. Mrs. Woolley, who appeared for defendant was given permission to speak but said she had nothing to say. This concluded the first case. John Henry Bradbury, 41, Winwick-road, was now charged with exposing 23 Pieces of beet, weighing 61Ibs. whicii were unfit for human rood, for sale in his shop, on March 315t—Mr.A.Br0wee appeared for the defence. Defendant carried on busi- ness at 41, Winwick.read, and on the 31st March In- spector Norman found exposed fur sale in defendant's shop window, 29 pieces ut meat weighing 6llbs, which were unsound. Ihe same preceediugs were taken in this case, and they had reason to believe that this meat was part of the same carcase, from which the other meat came. Witness thought it was not above a day since the animal was siaugh ' tered. Defendant adulated supplying Woolley with a round of beef off the same beast. The meat was certified unsound by Dr. Gornall, and Mr. Fergusson. The same day defendant and his father came to witness' office and examined the meat. Bradbury, senior, said that the meat must not be destroyed as he was going to bring a competent inspector to examine it, Witness promised to keep the meat until 6 p.m. the next day. By Mr. Browne :fle was prepared to say that this was not the same carcase that he saw the previous Friday. Mr, Browne : You don't allege that it was diseased ? No, unsound. It not that inconsistent P—No, It might be on. sound if it had only been slaughtered an hour. Inspector Norman : Yes. Mr. Browne (calling a Mrs. Floyd to the front) :Is this the woman. Inspector Norman : I don't know. I had aemething else to do beside look at women's faces, Mr. Brown.: Is that a courteous answer for an offi- cial to give a solicitor in court ? THE INFIRMARY.

do not know whether the street in front of the doors of the Infirmary was intended for a play ground ; but I should imagine it was not from the fact that it is paved with wood to minimise the sound of traffic pass- ing through it. el play ground, however, it is in practice and no one seems to interfere with such a use of it. I pass through tbe street frequently but I have never yet seen a policeman in it to whom complaint might be made. To-day it was crowded for hours with gangs of youths and boys and girls enjoying themselves with tops, hoops, bicycles, and foot balls, and the noise they made must have been distressing to the inmates of the buildirm Its this name of our common humanity I pro- test against the outrage upon the sick and suffering. Nor was the uproar of to-day a new thing, but only a repetition of what goes on more or less throughout the week. Should this letter inset the eyes of the Chief Constable I trust something will be .done to abate the nuisance.—Yours truly,

HUMANITY.

WHAT SHOULD WE EAT IN SUMMER.

Sir,—There is a very general demand for knowledge as to what are the best foods to be eaten for health and strength and vigour. Men are asking themselves what it is that our sturdy ancestors lived upon, and what it is that the hardy Scotsman builds up his brawn and brain wen, and what it is that the stalwart Turkish porters and brilliant dashing Sikhs and Ghoorkas find to give them the grand stamina for endarance which they possess, and a constitution so sound that they laugh at, and readily recover from, wounds and lchses of blood, which would hopelessly kill off the average Englishman fed on his beef and bacon and potted meat! There is one fact which stands out clearly, and that is, that all these people eat wheat and oats and cheese and lentils, figs and raisins, and oranges and dates;that it is from fruit and grains and vegetables and milk and cheese,that their health and power and vigour come,and not from flesh foods which they but rarely taste. This:is a startling lesson for,our decadenta and dyspeptics, for our neurotics and our rheumatics to learn, that the pathway to health and vigour lies through the use of Natural Food, and not through artificial stimulants. Now it the time to commence the better way. Literature, cookery receipes, and information on this important subject will be ,gladly forwarded free of charge by the Secretary of the Vegetarian Federal Union, la, Ferriage don Street, E.C., or by myself, on receipt of stamped addressed wrapper.—Yours faithfully, J. H. DOWNES.

liusholme, 15, Leicester Road, East Finchley, London, N.

WARRINGTON RAGGED SCHOOL AND MISSION.

Sir,—We invite attention to the following statement. This school seems to us during its existence to have been doing a very useful and a very needful work.under not very favourable conditions, doing a work untouched previously in the town. After many discussions in corn- nrittee with the managers, it has been thought wise to arrange to buy the property where the school is held, so as to make additional acommodation and improve some of the existing conditions. This was done only after a careful survey of the town was made,to ascertain if there could be found a suitable place, either for purchase or rental. As nothing could be found without involving a large outlay, it has been thought wise to treat with the owner of the existing property, when the funds would seem to justify such a step. The managers have saved and out in the Bank money towards its purchase and have rotting in addition,which together with the sum saved, will amount to between MO and £7OO. As this is insufficient to justify the purchaseme venture to appeal to the public for help. Meetings have been held nearly evety night in the week and on Sunday we have some MO to 300 ragged children on the regis- ter, who meet afternoon and evening and who probably would not be accepted in any other Sunday school. If the purchase of the property be carried out, it would be In the hands of trustees duly elected, whose names and Character would justify public confidence. We trust the self denying work the managers have been doing and the efforts they have made to raise the lowest and the out cast, will lead those who have a desire for the good of the town, to respond with liberal donations as a sum of £6OO or £7OO is desirable to purchase and put the property into good condition.—Signed, J. CHARLTON PARR. R. A. NAYLOR. JOHN CROSFIELD.

ROBERt ()ARNE PT. P.9.—Donations will be received by Parr's Banking Company, Limited, (Warrington Branch), by the Hon. Treasurer, Mr. R. A. Naylor, Cuerden Flail, Warrington, or by any of the officials holding a book bearing the Ragged School stamp.

WAR.

Sir,—Of all the developments of human wickedness which the world knowe,War is, without doubt, the most atrocious, and destructive of life and property, and has not one feature to mitigate its horrors. Tnis being so it follows that the men who plan and the men who perform its awful behests are, for thl time being, acting in unison with the evil spirit which holy writ describes as a murderer from the beginning and the fell destroyer of the works of God. It has been computed that net less than sixteen thousand millions of human beings have been slaughtered in war since the beginning of the world ! Mortal thought and mortal language can neither grasp not depict the scenes of wickedness and carnage involved in this statement! Nor are the benighted aed degraded races of mankind who know not the Lord but. bow down to ode of wood and stone, the greatest sinners in the matter of war. They are cruel and destructive enough but they cannot compete with their more enlightened and civilised bre- Wren in other countries in the art and appliances of murder. It was reserved for the polished nations who build and endow churches and chapels, and sing hymns to Jesus, the Prince of Peace, to bring into requisition the deadliest weapons that a prostituted science could devise for the destruction of human life. At the present moment there are in Christian Europe millions of men armed !.th these weapons waiting the beck of some 'kingly or queenly despot to enter into horrible conflict and pollute God's lovely weed with the blood of frenzied murderers. Sooner or later that beck will be given, and then will ensue a slaughter of Christians by Christians that will astonish the world and send a thrill of infernal joy through the realms of darkness. I In the meantime this country continuos to exercise its warriors with some preliminary lighting on a small scale. Only a little while age the newspapers informed us day by day of the atrocities committed by a portion of our army on the weak and comparatively defenceless tribes on the North West frontier of India. These wild dwellers among their own mountains had a sense of independence, and would not quietly submit to foreign invasion; and the consequence was that they were shot down in hundreds, their property looted, their fruit and shelter trees dostroyed, their villages burned, and their wives and children left to die of cold and starvation. And, with deep shame be it said, all this was not the work of barbarians, but of the military servants of a people who pride themselves upon being the most civilised and christianised upon the earth ! But the scene has changed. There is a lull on the Indian frontier. The fires of hell have died out there in the meantime ; but only to blaze out with more tereible force and effect in another r 4 ion. • few days ago the Press reported a great victory for the British arms in the Soudan, no less than three thousand Dervishes having been killed right out in a very brief space of time. It was more a butchery than a battle, for the undisciplined and badly armed children of the desert were no match for the highly-trained soldiers and death dealing weapons of her Gracious Majesty. The scene after the conflict must have been heartrending aud horrible I beyond description. And yet in the very midst of it, surrounded by heaps of dead and dying men and animals, three priests, an Anglican. a Romanist, and a Presbyterian, were found who Were not ashamed to insult the name they bore by performing what they impiously ealled a thanksgiving service for the victory. Their prayers and praises should have been addressed to Moloch, and not to the Divine Being whose name is Love. And quite in keeping with the blood-thirsty spirit of the service was the conduct of" the head of the Church," members of Parliament and others of the gentlemen of England who live at theme in ease, who telegraphed their thanks and congratulations to the ringleaders in the slaughter. With such awful facts as these staring us in the face I think it is time we made a little lowering of our reli- gious claims. It is true we have plenty of the article called modern Christianity amongst us, but alas, the gentle, loving, forgiving Spirit of Jesus seems to be rather in the background at present. If we made Jesus our model,as we profess to do, we should neither provide men nor money to gratify the lust of our rulers for em- pire,nor allow the Christian name to be dishonoured by deeds of blood and robbery on our part as we now do in every quarter of the world.—Yours truly, lONA.

WARRINGTON RAGGED SCHOOL AND MISSION.

MRS. G. S. REANEY, IN PARR HALL.

A special effort in aid of the funds of the Warringtoo Ragged School and Mission was made on Sunday, when public services were held, afternoon and evening in Parr Hall. The attendance on neither occasion was as large as we should have liked to see, coneieering the good and philanthropic work which is done by the Ragged School, but in the evening there was a fair cesegregetion.Mrs.G. S. Reaney,of Greenwich (wife of the Rev. G. S. Rainey, formerly of Wycliffe Church, Warrington), was the speaker at both services, the chair being occupied by Mr. R. Garnett, J.P. The eloquent and earnest language In which Mrs. Heaney addressed her hearers rivitted their close atteation. In the evening Mrs.Reaney delivered an impressive address on Paul's first Epistle to the Thessalonians, taking the words, "Followers. Examples,Echoes," as her teat. There was, she continued, a great deal of sham Christianity now, and there were many who had the name of Christian only, audit was this class or people that did harm to religion. It was not the atheist, it was not the sceptic, not the general method of trying to pull down the seronghold of their faith, which did the harm, so much as these who bore Christ's nAllle and yet were really out- side the fold. There was a great deal of artificial Christianity and this would never maze them followers of Jesus Christ. To be followers of Christ day by day they must tike Cerist'a standard as their ,awn. She urged people to be reel, 6...c:ere, thorough, expreesing the hope that the life of the people of Warrington would be et such a character that it would bring gladness to all around them. In the siourse of her address Mrs.Reaney related several incidents which had come under her ob- servation, and which illustrated the beauty and helpful- noes of real Christi 'laity. Before the collection was taken, Mr. Garnett said he felt thankful ter the privileee of hearing Mrs. Heaney that ovening.lt was a matter for thankfulness tis all that there were many in the town who had tried to do some practical service for the Mester in reclaiming and train- ing a 'action of the rising generation at present outside almost any other inflaences. While there was much self-imposed poverty, improvidence and all its kindred eviliethere seemed te be no agen 3y in Warringten deal- ing with the destitute and lower portion ef the people. One of the most painful things to him was the apple. - ance of parents at (loan, time after time for neglecting to send their children to school. Where the weakness was and how it was to be remedied be did net know. Ile had often had Much Milieus thought, and seeeral consultations with minis- ters and clergy of various churches to see if they could not help in the matter. He thought it weuld be a very good thing if they had a ragged day school aed could get the children driven into it. But in the ab- sence of all agencies of that sort they were thankful to know that there was an agency on foot which had for many years done substantial good. It had not made a pared* of itself but qaite a number of workingmen had taken it in hand and done a service to the town, which the town ought to be very thankful for. Fla would urge that those et their friends who were preeent and no doubt Sympathised with the movement, but who could not give personal service should take the opportunity now, of testifying their admiration of the riceerne and their willingness to support it by a very liberal Contri- bution. On the plattorm,at each service,were about a hundred Ragged Scholl children,who with the support ot a band, conducted by Mr. R. Wood, led the sineine witis great enthusiasm. For Such mites as some of them were,their knowledge of the tune was ,e,rcellent arid testified to the careful training they had received.

SAD DEATH Or MISS PENNINGTON, NEWTON.

An inquest was held at the Pied Bull Hutel,New- ton on Saturday, by Mr. S. Brighouse, coroner, into the circumstances attending the death of Miss Mary Pennington, 59 years of age, of Park-road, Newton, retired school mistress. It appeared from the evi- dence adduced that the deceased about a fortnight age slipped when descending the stairs on the second landing,and broke both bones in one of her legs. She was attended by Dr. Watkins, and was progressing very favourably until last Sunday week, when she had a relapse The doctor was immediately summoned, but she never recovered, and gradually sank, dying on Thursday night last week, the cause of death being syncope. The jury returned a ver- dict of "Accidental death." The deceased lady with her sister have been residents of Newton for many years, and up to about 18 meatus ago were associ- ated with a lady's school which was conducted with unqualified success, many excellent pupils being finished and turned out. Both ladies were held in very great reapect,and the sad end of one of them has evoked much sympathy. CONGREGATIONAL SUNDAY SCHOOL, NEWTON. The annual special services in aid of the above school were held in the Congregational Church on Sunday. There ware good congregations at both morning and evening services,at which appropriate sermons were preached by the Rev. Principal Mitchell, 8.A., of Nottingham. Special anthems were sung by the choir, Mr. A. Knight presiding at the organ. In the afternoon,a musical service was given in the schoolroom by members of the choir, and a choir of school children, conducted by the organist Mr. A. Knight, when the following pro- gramme was very efficiently submitted :—Aathem "0 give thanks," choir; solo "A dream of Paradise" Mr. R. Powell;solo, "Abide with me," Miss Mather; chorus "Onward Christian Soldiers," children and choir. The collections throughout the day amounted to £27. HOPE OF EARLEBTOWN LODGE, LO G.T. In the Co-operative Hall on Monday evening the above lodge, held their meeting, a good gathering being present. Bro. Cutler CT. was in the chair, and three candidates were admitted to rnemberehie and one was re-obligated. The secretary read several communications dealing with the Order, and the C.T. read a letter from Sister Grant thanking the lodge for sympathy shown to her in her late illness. Bro. E. Newman then gave the report of the Con- vention held at Leigh on Saturday. and stated that Earleetown' Ashton and Warrington were doing a grand work in increasing the membership, and that the next place of meeting would be at Ashton. INDEPENDENT LASOUR PARTY,EARLBETOWN. Under the auspices of the Earlestown branch a lecture was given in the Co-operative Hall on Mon- day evening by Mr, J, Bruce Glassier on " Men who have risen; Jack Sheppard and J. Gould." Mr. Wright, Warrington, presided, and said there were plenty of men and women who ought to have been in that room instead of crowding round street corners. That night they were all looking round and asking themselves continuously what was Socialism doing. Was it making any progress? Were they getting tired of the great uphill fight or were they continually putting on the armour of Socialism and preparing to go on with the fight? At the Birmingham Conference, they showed those people who said that the I.L.P. was dead or dying that they were very much alive and that Socialism was going to fight, and fight to win. Be believed that in certain constituencies where Socialism was progressing, the seats would be fought and neither the Liberal or Conservative party would be con- sulted as to how and where they were to fight. (Hear, hear.)—Comrade Glasier, in the course of his lecture endeavoured to show by comparison that there was no material difference between Jack Sheppard, who robbed the rich to get rich himself, and Jay Gould who rubbed the poor to enrich himself. He gave a brief outline of the principles Of Socialism, and urged his views strongly upon his bearers. EARLESTOWN WOMEN'S CO-OPERATIVE GUILD. ' On Tuesday evening in connection with the above a lecture was given in the Co-operseive Hall. by Miss Mayo, of Huddersfield, on" Cooperation.' Mr. W. Clare presided,and there was a fair audience Miss Mayo dwelt at some length upon the career of the pioneers of Co-operation who commenced oper- ations in Rochdale, observing that amongst the 28 Pioneers one was a woman,and that woman did her fair share of the work in helping forward the movement. The movement, she said, affected the women as much as the man, and in some societies there were more women than men. In the Society ahe belonged to the women never knew that they might go to the quarterly meetings until a guild was started, but when they found out that, as shareholders, they were as much entitled to take part in the meetings as the men, they went, and voted as they considered best. Seeing that women had the power she contended they ought to know how their money was going to be spent. She insis- ted that Co-operative women ought not to buy any- thing hut what was made and manufactured by the Co-operative Society. Cs-operation provided that a fair wage should be given to those who supplis,i things to the Stores, and 50,000 or 60.000 men and women were engaged up and down the country manufacturing goods for sale at the Storee. There was no Pens", she said, in keeping a shop and buy- ing their goods at the shops of private traders. Every co-operator should bee that every single thing they wore and ate, and what. they had in their houses, had get the letters " C.W.S." upon thein,and should see that they did not buy goods provided by the sweater( At the close a vote of thanks was accor- ded Miss Mayo for her address.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN

PT AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT.] The way in which the sanitary department in this district deal with the ash pit refuse calls forth serious comment. Only the other day I noticed a man behind a long row of houses hauling out boxes of refuse from their receptacles,and leaving them exposed to the wind and sun for sonte length of time,before the cart came round to gather them up. What made it appear worse,all along the row the women were performing the weekly wash and their lines were filled with snow white clothes. The men,howerer, cared nothing for this, and when the cart arrived the boxes were emptied into it,and the dust was carried by the wind in all directions,chiefly upon the clothing referred to. Women, generally, are not given to swearing,but they used very strong language on this occasion,aud the men engaged had a fairly warm time of it. I think matters could be arranged a little better than this,and a more suita- ble time chosen for emptying the boxes.

At the annual meeting of tl.e Council on Monday, Mr. Borron was again appointed chairman for the next 12 months. This is just as it ought to be, for there are many things under the consideration of the Council which should be dealt with under the chairmanship of Mr. Borron. The most important of these, perhaps, is the water question which has been on the carpet for some length of time now. Mr. Borron says the committee will be prepared to lay their scheme before the Cornell shortly. It will be an interesting document no doubt.

What do the Council intend doing in regard to the proposed visit of 11.ELEI. Prince of Wales to Gate- wood on the 17th May next? They are careful to keep all the information regarding their intentions to themselves. I suppose the usual waste paper in the form of an address will be presented to the Prince, and there will be the usual congratulations and welcome,always supposing that the Prince will accept one. What a honour for our worthy coun- cillors! Nobody can tell what may happen, one or two may possibly be knighted on the spot—Per- haps !

ELECTION OF CHAIRMLN.

The first business before the meeting was to elect a chairman for the ensuing year. Mr. Stone said he had the greatest pleasure in propos- posing Mr. Herron as chairman for the year. It was quite unnecessary for him to add any words of his in re- commendation, as Mr. &won's service; sufficiently re- commended him to the members present,both in his ca- pacity as chairman of the Board and as a member of the various committees. He had served in every position with unremitting attention, and he always took the deepest interest in wnatever business was before him. He (Mr. Stone) thoueht he would not only be expressing the sentiments of the Council but of every rateoayer,in proposing that Mr. Horton again occupy the chair. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Colliegwool seconded, and endorsed what Mr. Stone had said. As vioe-ohairman he bad been brought in close contact with Mr. B)rron, and he was confident that they eaulu not do better than re-elect him. Hader all circumstances, in the present condition of affairs and with the various schemes in process, they could not 05 better advised than in asking eir.Borron to stand again. (Applause.) Mr. Emmett said he supported the resolution for one or two reasons. The first reason was that there were in preparation the water scheme,the gas works extensions, and the street making,which had had a great deal of the attention of the Council, but which, so far as his know- ledge went. had none of them been really carried out yet. When they should come to the end of them re- mained for this Council or some other Council to see. Three years ago it was promised by several members of the Board, that they were going to have water at half the present prices, streete were going to be almost paved with gold in comparison to what they had been. With reeard to the gas works, well, he did not know what they were going to have there but some reformation from previous years. Seeing that Mr. Marron had had these matters in hand, and would perhaps during his next period of office be able to see some of them completed, these were the speaker's reasons for supporting the election of Mr. Her- ron. He might just say with regard to the water scheme, that a good many complaints had been made as to the scarcity of water, but this was not due to the London and North-Western Railway. Some 18 months ago Mr. Webb offered to lay a new main to Newton Bridge in order to give the Council a better supply of water. The Council then began to form some other scheme and was going in to supply the town with water at less price. They had not arrivad at that stags yet and therefore any fault rested entirely with the Council and not with the Railway Company. The election was unanimously confirmed. Mr. Borten then took the chair and tetanked the mem- bers for again electing him to the office, which,he might say, was no sinecure. He thought himself that three years was a very long period te serve but he was quite willing and agreeable to devote another year to the ser- vices of this Council. With reference to the various points rtientirmed by Mr. Emmett he might say that although publicly little or nothing might be seen actively being done with the various schemes brought forware for the advancement of the district, they had been con- etantly and attentively worked out by sub-committees. Hs thought that with regard to the Gas Works the com- mittee Was prepared to lac a full scheme before them, and which would increase the production of the Gee Works to double the present capacity. This would meet all requirements for many years to come. With regard to the water, as Mr. Emmett bad explained, they had had many complaints of the deficient supply. The committee having this matter in hand had about completed all its work, and would at an early date,—within the next month ptebsbly—be in a position to lay their scheme before the Council. The work of the Highway's Committee had been retarded, with regard to street making, by the Local Government bard. This district was no exception to the difficul- ties which were thrown in the way by the Board upon districts of this nature. There were many urban dis- tricts in Lancashire and other parts of the country, which were retarded in their work of street making and improvements by the Local Government Board,owing to sewerage schemes. What that sewerage scheme in their CUE was, no one seemed yet to have ascertained, and it would be a difficult matter for the Chairman of the Highways Committee to adopt some scheme that would be advantageous to the district and also fulfil the re- quircinents of the Local Government Board. There was nothing he need say further, but he wished all these schemes to be thrashed out. Full information would be given the Council and he hoped that this year would not only see the commencement of these scheme; but also their completion. He again thanked the Council for the honour they had done him in placing him in the chair, and he would endeavour to uphold the dignities of the office in the bast possible manner. (Hear, hear, and applause).

ST. JAMES' CHURCH, HAYDOCK. AN OPEN AIR VESTRY MEETING.

It will be remenbered that last week after the pro- ceedings in connection with the vestry meeting in the schoolroom in connection with St. James' Church, Hay. desk, an adjo Jrnment was made for a week,the meeting te be held in the church vestry. Shortly before seven o'clock' Mr. John Robinson, parishioners' warden,with a number of the parishioners, assembled at the church,but they found all the doers lacked. Accordingly the meet- ing was held in the churchyard, Mr. Robinson taking up a position on the vestry steps, and presiding over the gathering. He said the meeting had been called by proper and legal notice at their request last Thursday nicht. He read the notice, which was dated April leth, 18911 and signed "John Robinson, parishioners' warden." The notice was duly posted on the church door on Sun- day morning last, and was seen by a large number of the people who were present there that night. Think- ing that some mischievous individual might take the nstice dawn he wrote out a mend one,and requested one of his sidesmen to take it to the church at six o'clock on Sunday evening and post it in the event of the first notice having been taken down, They found the first notice had been taken by someone, but were unable to say by whom. They posted the second notice and Mr. Cunliffe at 8.15 came into the porch, saw the notice and took it down. He thought they would agree with him that the conduct of Mr. P. Cunliffe was very reprehen- sible ; it Was extremely bad taste on his part to have taken down the notice. At any rate, he (Mr. Robinson) was satisfied the notice was a properly legal notice, pro- perly posted and had fulfilled all its requirements. The taking down of the notice only showed the bitter ani- mosity of the Vicar and his friends. They came there that night to hold that meeting, and they found every door locked In spite of the fact that the churchwardens were expected to see that nothing of an unseemly char- acter took place in the church, Mr. Ireland thought it right and proper to have the doors locked. He, Mr. Robinson, had been to the present cars-taker and church cleaner, who said that she had given the keys to somebody else and she did not know where they were.—A voice " Mr. Ireland is in the school."— Mr. Robinson said he had heard that before. He had been to the school and found every door locked. Last Tuesday week Mr P. Cunliffe waited upo3 him and de- sired him to fill up the Archdeacon's paper, which was to certify that the Vicar had properly conducted the ser- vices of the church, and had properly hahited himself whilst performing the services, and in addition the war dens were asked to say whether there was anything else they would like to mention. Mr Cunliffe and himself spent two hours over it, and filled it ill honestly and truthfully, and to the best of their ability, and after two hours Mr. Cunliffe signed it along with himself. The same night, Mr. Cunliffe met him in the street, and said he had no right to sign tho paper, and that he burnt it. (" Shame "). When they put all those facts together he thought they would come to the conclusion that it was about time the law was altered so that those people who kept and maintained the Established Church could have a solid voice in conducting the church in a proper man- ner. (Hear, bear). lie had received a notice from Mr. Hilton that he intended to move at the adjourned meet- ing a vote of protest against illegal ornaments and cere- monies practised in Haydock Parish Church. Mr. Hilton moved the foregoing, and Mr. J. T. Fair. huret seconded, and it was carried unanimously. Mr. I. Wright understood that the Archdeacon's paper Was burnt, and if that was 80 he had great pleasure in 'moving that another be filled up by the peoples' warden and signed by the sidesnaen and any other respectable parishioner, and sent to theßishcp. There were a lot of illegalities practised in the Church. Mr. Stevens seconded, and the motion was carried. The meeting then proceeded wi' h the election of parishioner's warden, sidesmen, &1., and resulted as follow :—Mr. Jahn Robinson, parishioner's warden; sidemen, Messrs. J. T. Fairhurst, J. Hilton, T. Stevens, E. Jenkins, W. Smailes ; siclesmen for St. Alban's Mis- sion, Messrs. Joseph Richardson, E. Norcross. I. Wright, Joseph Littler, R. Gregory ; lay representatives to the Diocesan Conference, Messrs. Robinson and Hilton. A vote of thanks having been accorded the Chairman, the meeting dispersed.

TFIE FINAL FOR TtlE WARRINGTON

CUP TICTORY OF NEWTON.

More than usual interest was taken in the final tie for the above cup which was played on the ground of St, Elphin's, Dalton Bank, Warrington, on Saturday after- noon. There was an attendance of nearly 4,000. Both teams had a large number of supporters. Mr. Lewis, of Penketh, was the referee, and his decisions gave every satisfaction.

John Foster kicked off and the Cheshire men by clever passing were the first to attack. Laithwaite cleared, but another led by Bailey and Marsh effected a fine clearance. Good passing by Bell was taken up by Houghtontand though Stanley checked the Newton cen- tre when he looked dangerous another raid led by Lee was instrumental in fercing Tideswell to concede a corner. Newt on were now playing with considerable dash, and after " hands " hid been conceded against Houghten,Newton by superior tacties get within measurable distance of Tideswell. Stanley was again prominent with corns powerful kicking, but Rase was weak, and Hayes feeding Morrisay judiciously the Wit- ton men had again to fall back. A sharp visit was paid to the Nowtea ,ad, and Marsh dealt with a snot by Kettle. Lee and Butler responded, and Tideswell had little chance with an oblique shot sent in by Morrisey. Stimulated by this success Newton again made matters warm. Tideswell was called upon to deal with shots from Houghton and Hill. Witton next had a turn, but their shooting at close quarters was very erratic, and Marsh had little difficulty in deal- ing with the shots sent in by Done and Foster. Marsh was again the saviour of his side in dealing with a hot fusilade of the Cestrians, and Foster shot widely behind. Newton passed well and Butler almost scored from a long shot. Witton again got dawn just before the in- terval, but Marsh cleared splendidly, and at half-time Newton led by one goal to nil. The second half opened in sensational style, for imme- diately on the restart the Witton centre placed the aids on equal terms. Newton respanded,and from a"bully" lU front of goal Rowbotham gave the Lancashire men the lead. Nothing daunted,the Wittonians played up pluckily and a speedy run by Joe Foster culminated in Marsh having little chance with a shot from Done's foot. N‘sw- ton sacred twice in quick succession, and though Wit- ten made a gallant effort to get on equal terms, it was not until a minute before the cessation of hostilities that Marsh was defeated. Nesatna defended well, and the final result was Newton-le-Willows, four goals ; Witton Albion, three goals. A PROTEST LODGED. Since the match, Witton Albion have lodged a protest with the Warrington and District Association on the ground that Richard Lee. one of the Newton players, was ineligible. The protest will be considered to-night (Friday) at a meeting of the committe in the Roebuck I Hotel, Bridge-street.

THE CHRISTIAN BRETHRRN, EARLESTOWN, On Tuesday night a tea and lantern lecture was held in the Meeting Room of the above Society as a wind up of the season of the Mutual Improvement Class. About 30 eat down to an excellent tea the tables being presided over by Miss Eckersley, Mrs. Siret,and Mrs. Broster. After tea the room was cleared and a very interesting lecture was given by jr. Crook,of Wigan, on" Burmah ; the religion and customs of its people," illustrated by limelight views. Mr. Harrison introduced the lecturer and said that Mr.Croek had been 400 miles up the Irra- waddy river to locate the missionaries sent out by the Christian Brethren. A hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Crook,the lecturer, and also to Mr. J. U. Graham, who had charge of the lantern.
